Instalco · INSTAL-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 19 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Instalco memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Instalco was written: 55,784 words, against 9,742 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 26,867 words · written 22 October 2026, 11:46 to 12:12 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Instalco (INSTAL-SE): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 22 October 2026 by the Investment Analyst, for the Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years).

Read this first: the basis of every figure below.

  • The Q3 2026 report lands tomorrow, 23 October 2026. Every answer is pre-Q3. Where a Q3 outcome would change a judgement, the answer names the threshold, so the Consumer can update on the print rather than re-derive.
  • Money is SEK millions unless stated. Three profit bases appear and must not be mixed: the company's adjusted EBITA (FY2025 SEK 870m, 6.4 percent; reported EBITA 5.9 percent after SEK 75m of items affecting comparability); FactSet's operating EBITDA (FY2025 SEK 1,092m, 8.0 percent); and the brokers' adjusted EBITDA (FY2025 SEK 1,229m, 9.0 percent), the only basis on which consensus (FY2026 to FY2028) can be read. Consensus EPS (brokers' basis, FY2025 SEK 1.76; FY2026 SEK 2.28, FY2027 SEK 3.01, FY2028 SEK 3.42) is read before amortisation of acquired intangibles; reported basic EPS for FY2025 was SEK 1.28. Consensus rests on three brokers (DNB Carnegie Buy SEK 47, Pareto Buy SEK 48, Nordea Hold SEK 45 dated 19 October 2026); write "the three brokers expect", not "consensus expects", wherever precision matters.
  • Leverage appears on two definitions: the company's net debt to EBITDA (2.6x at Q2 2026, excluding leases and earn-outs; the 2.5x target is on this basis) and FactSet's (2.82x, including SEK 505m of lease liabilities). The bank covenant basis excludes contingent earn-outs (DNB Carnegie).
  • Price SEK 42.52 (21 October 2026); 24-month-forward P/E 12.9x, the 46th percentile of the stock's own ten-year history; the latest memo (6 October 2026) parks the stock on return (modal 9.3 percent a year) with a buy level of about SEK 37.

External change forces

C5510 questions
C-0160C-0247C-0352C-0458C-0546C-0668C-0736C-0854C-0948C-1057

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external forces are a cyclical turn, not an inflection. The Swedish building cycle has turned, the backlog proves it, and the public and renovation floor is higher than in the last downturn; but visibility is 12 to 18 months, the Q2 and Q3 2026 organic rates are flattered by trough comparatives, installation revenue lags starts by two to four quarters so the broad recovery reaches the income statement in 2027, and the recovery is happening into a Riksbank hiking cycle that acts first on Instalco's financing cost and multiple. Pricing relief is real but borrowed from competitors' data-centre capacity, which is itself exposed to the artificial-intelligence financing cycle. For the model: organic growth high-single-digit for two more quarters then fading to mid and low single digits; no price in the margin bridge; wage inflation from spring 2027; net financial items not falling as fast as the three brokers assume; currency a 1 to 2 percent reported drag.

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, against the last 12 to 18 months.

C-01

End-market demand: structural or cyclical change, and with how much visibility?

C60

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement: a cyclical turn in Sweden, now about one year old, with 12 to 18 months of visibility, not the two years the question sets as the bar for a cyclical swing to count; structural overlays (the renovation directive, defence, public health, data centres) lengthen the tail beyond 2028 but do not drive 2027. Model demand as early-cycle recovery that fades, not as a structural step-up.

The reasoning, in order of weight:

  • The Swedish building cycle has turned and the evidence is now broad rather than anecdotal: building starts (Byggstartsindikatorn) +8.0 percent year on year in July 2026, non-residential starts +13.2 percent, residential +7.5 percent; construction sentiment 102.9 (above normal) and household confidence 102.2, the highest since the 2021 peak (Nordea, 29 September 2026); housing transactions +12 percent to 46,500 units and house prices +3.4 percent (Jefferies, September 2026). Instalco's own series confirms it: organic growth -2.8, -3.6, +5.0, +4.9, +8.4 percent over the five quarters to Q2 2026, after six consecutive quarters of contraction; backlog +17.0 percent to SEK 10,937m (+14.8 percent organic), 1.21x last-twelve-month installation sales, the highest backlog per employee since Q1 2023.
  • But the mechanism that produced the Q2 acceleration is partly arithmetic. Installation is late-cyclical: revenue lags construction starts by several quarters (Deutsche Bank on Bravida; Morgan Stanley puts the lag at six to twelve months). The prior-year comparatives were the trough (-2.8 percent in Q2 2025, -3.6 percent in Q3 2025), and DNB Carnegie expects Swedish organic growth of about 12 percent in Q3 2026 explicitly on "easier comparables" plus the metropolitan projects (Stockholm, Gothenburg, Malmö) moving from tendering into execution. So the headline organic rate for the next two quarters overstates the underlying demand slope, and the comparatives harden from Q1 2027 (Q1 2026 was +4.9 percent, Q2 2026 +8.4 percent).
  • Visibility is the company's own figure: contracts are typically secured 12 to 18 months ahead and the backlog equals 77 percent of last-twelve-month sales. That is good visibility for a contractor and short of two years. Nothing in the files gives Instalco a view into 2028 demand other than the structural overlays.
  • The structural overlays are real and dated, but their dates sit beyond the model's near horizon. The recast Energy Performance of Buildings Directive requires renovation of the worst-performing 16 percent of non-residential buildings by 2030 and 26 percent by 2033 and a 16 percent cut in residential primary energy use by 2030; no member state met the 29 May 2026 transposition deadline, infringement proceedings opened against all 27 on 15 July 2026, Sweden filed its draft renovation plan in June 2026 and expects implementation guidelines by end-2026, with building-law changes phased July to October 2026. DNB Carnegie's reading, which I adopt, is that enforcement could lift European renovation rates toward 3 percent a year from the 1 percent baseline "after 2027". Sweden's 2026 defence budget rises SEK 26bn to SEK 175bn (2.8 percent of GDP, 3.1 percent by 2028); Norway's Helse Sør-Øst plans about NOK 73.8bn of hospital capital spending in 2027 to 2030; the Swedish transport plan 2026 to 2037 is SEK 1,171bn. These underpin the public share of Instalco's work (9 to 20 percent of sales depending on the cut; schools and hospitals 16 percent) and make the floor under demand higher than in 2023 to 2025, but they are not accelerating in the next six months.
  • Two things cap the swing. First, the Riksbank has "materially strengthened its tightening signal": a 25 basis point hike in November 2026 "looks increasingly likely" (Barclays, Nordea), Deutsche Bank projects 1.75 percent to 2.50 percent by mid-2027, and Goldman Sachs rank Sweden among the most rate-sensitive developed economies because mortgages reset. The demand turn is happening against rising rates, the opposite of 2020 to 2021. Second, private residential new-build remains "extremely sluggish" (Instalco's own words), Finnish residential starts are frozen, and Norway's recovery is confined to Oslo and the south with Norges Bank at 4.50 percent after a September 2026 hike. Instalco's direct new-residential exposure is only 7 percent of sales (from 14 percent), which limits the drag but also means the housing-led part of the recovery passes it by.
  • The one figure that argues for a longer runway is DNB Carnegie's estimate that Swedish installation volumes sit about 15 percent below normalised levels. If right, volume can rise for two to three years before pricing tightens. I treat it as the upper bound of the cone, not the modal path, because it is one broker's estimate and the rate path leans against it.

Implication for the model: organic growth high-single-digit for H2 2026 (comparatives), mid-single-digit in 2027, low-to-mid single-digit in 2028, with the public and renovation mix holding the floor and the rate path setting the ceiling. Consensus revenue (+11.7 percent FY2026, +13.0 percent FY2027, +4.0 percent FY2028) embeds Fabri (about SEK 1.3bn annualised from August 2026) on top of this; strip it out before judging the organic assumption.

C-02

Competitive intensity: changing to what extent, and in which direction?

❌ D47

Question asked To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

Judgement: intensity peaked in 2024 to 2025 and is easing in direction, but from a level that is still elevated and that remains elevated in the segment Instalco occupies (mid-sized, price-led tenders in Sweden). The easing is volume-driven, so it is as fragile as the recovery.

  • The peak: Swedish installation tenders "routinely attract fifteen price-cutting bidders" against a historical baseline of five (ARE Sverige expert call, February 2025, re-cited through October 2026); Swedish public authorities "heavily prioritise the lowest price" (former Ramboll regional director, January 2026); southern Sweden stayed a "weak geographic pocket where depressed private construction continues to squeeze installation margins" (Deutsche Bank, May 2026); privately owned Nimlas and Assemblin Caverion out-earned Instalco Sweden on margin through the first three quarters of 2025 (DNB Carnegie); even Bravida, the margin-over-volume exemplar, "admitted to compromising on this strategy due to intense competitive bidding". The Swedish plumbing sector saw elevated bankruptcies, and 8.5 percent of Nordic companies are in financial distress (Alvarez & Marsal, 2026).
  • The easing: Instalco's Q2 2026 report says the volume of incoming projects "has stabilised the pricing environment" after quarters when overcapacity forced aggressive underbidding; Konjunkturinstitutet specialised-construction price expectations have been positive since August 2025 after declining since May 2022; DNB Carnegie notes that Bravida's absorption of technical labour into large data-centre contracts (order intake +44 percent in Q2 2026, SEK 4.3bn Green Mountain) "improves overall installer pricing power" for everyone else; and distressed small competitors are exiting or selling.
  • Why it stays elevated for Instalco specifically: its chosen project band (SEK 1m to SEK 75m, mid-sized) is exactly where the 25,000 small firms and the newbuild contractors pivoting into renovation compete, and where public buyers are price-led. The merged Assemblin Caverion (about SEK 41bn of revenue) "did not ease the aggressive bidding behaviour in Swedish tender processes" (ARE Sverige). Nimlas's "4-40-4" plan (SEK 40bn of sales by 2031, doubling in Sweden, Finland and Norway) and InstallatørGruppen's 48 acquisitions since 2023 add well-funded consolidators to the bidding and to the market for targets.
  • A data gap the Consumer should carry: Swedish developers say they request two to three installers for typical private sections, which contradicts the fifteen-bidder claim (the key-question research flags this). The honest reading is that public and large-contractor tenders are crowded and relationship-led private work is not; Instalco's 80 percent repeat-customer share sits in the second.
C-03

Disruption force: strengthening or disproved, and acting in which direction?

C52

Question asked To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

Judgement: no disruption force is being disproved and none is strong enough to move the next 18 months of financials; the forces at work (building automation, prefabrication, digital design and procurement, artificial intelligence in administration) favour scaled installers over the fragmented tail, and within the scaled group they favour the centralised model (Bravida) over Instalco's decentralised one. Net effect on Instalco: slightly positive on demand, slightly negative on relative position, immaterial to the near-term model.

  • Direction on demand: technical installations have risen to about 45 percent of construction budgets as digitalisation and integrated systems add content; smart HVAC and artificial-intelligence-led controls cut building energy use by up to 30 percent; the Energy Performance of Buildings Directive mandates building automation in non-residential stock. This upweights the installer, not the disruptor.
  • Direction on position: Bravida runs 5D building information modelling, a proprietary purchasing platform and a cloud service and has scaled building automation to SEK 1bn of sales; Instalco's answer is organic start-ups, Intec (about 500 consultants since 2020, margins above group) and Inmatiq (building automation, launched May 2024, first quarter without an earnings drag in Q1 2026, Siemens and Schneider partnerships, a SEK 30m Region Blekinge framework). The research's own counterpoint is the right one: a federation of 150 subsidiaries "struggles to standardise technology", and mandating platforms risks the entrepreneurial autonomy the model depends on. Nordic tradesman software penetration is only 25 to 30 percent, so the gap is between consolidators, not between consolidators and the tail.
  • Prefabrication (30 to 40 percent faster completion, 60 percent fewer site deliveries, minimal capital) is a productivity lever any subsidiary can adopt; it is not a threat to the installer stage.
  • Modelling implication: treat Inmatiq as an option (the earnings review's 50 to 100 basis points of group margin by 2028 if it scales like Intec) and not a base-case line; treat the digital gap as a reason Instalco's margin ceiling is its own 8 percent history and not Burkhalter's or SPIE's.
C-04

Political or regulatory change: how far under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C58

Question asked To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

Judgement: materially supportive and dated, but the dates fall mostly beyond 18 months; within the near horizon the regulatory channel is a floor under public demand rather than an accelerator. The only near-dated negative is monetary, not regulatory.

  • Under way: the recast Energy Performance of Buildings Directive (dates in C-01); Sweden's "Villaeffekten" scheme from 1 September 2026 (30 percent of eligible material costs up to SEK 60,000 per small house, SEK 300m a year to 2030), partly offset by the ROT deduction reverting to 30 percent from 50 percent in 2026; Klimatklivet subsidies for heat pumps and district heating; green lending frameworks tying bond eligibility to energy class A or B (Intea, June 2026). Defence: Sweden SEK 175bn in 2026, Norway +NOK 115bn by 2036. Germany, now relevant through Fabri: the GModG building energy act from July 2026 with staged life-cycle assessments from 2028, and BEG funding doubling eligible costs to EUR 60,000 for single-family homes.
  • How it bears: it makes the non-discretionary share of demand larger each year, which is why the research calls Instalco's and Bravida's 35 percent exposure to structural renovation "middle tier" purity. It does not create Instalco-specific volume in the next two quarters.
  • Costs and frictions: Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive compliance raises administrative overhead for decentralised units (double materiality, Scope 1 to 3 from FY2025); Swedish enforcement against work-related crime raises compliance pressure in labour-intensive trades; Norway's coalition government collapsed over implementing EU energy directives, a live example of transposition slipping. The sector digest's rule applies: "a renovation law in infringement lengthens the tail; it does not drive next year."
  • The negative: the Riksbank's November 2026 hike signal, which is the single regulatory-policy item that bears on 2027 demand and on Instalco's financing cost (C-07, C-76).
C-05

Input costs, availability and supplier concentration: net headwind or tailwind?

❌ D46

Question asked How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

Judgement: a mild net headwind, with the sign set by labour, not materials. Materials are currently a slight tailwind (wholesalers absorbing cost, heat-pump oversupply), metals a watch item, and the 2027 Swedish wage round the one input that can move the 2027 margin by itself. Supplier concentration is a structural squeeze that does not change inside the horizon.

  • Labour: Swedish collective agreements settle at about 3.2 percent a year for 2026 and 2027, total wage growth 3.2 to 3.4 percent (Swedbank, Riksbank), hourly wages 3.5 percent in 2026 easing to 3.3 percent in 2027 (Nordea). Against that, SEB expects agreed minimum wages to rise 8 percent for 2027, and local wage drift for scarce technicians "routinely exceeds collective limits" (Sweco expert). Employee benefit costs were 36.8 percent of net sales in FY2025 (35.9 percent in FY2024). The spring 2027 round lands on contracts priced in 2026; Swedish contracts lack the automatic indexation Danish AB18 contracts carry.
  • Materials: materials and purchased services were 46.9 percent of sales in FY2025 (materials about 40 percent of costs, subcontractors about 16 percent). Copper forecasts rose to $12,800 a tonne and aluminium to $3,400 on Middle East supply shocks (Deutsche Bank, April to May 2026); Geberit took 1 percent price in Q2 2026. Offsets: wholesaler Solar "struggled to pass on salary inflation and faced a lack of supplier price increases", so distributors are absorbing (DNB Carnegie, August 2026); the European heat-pump market is oversupplied with about 10 percent price pressure on older refrigerant products (Morgan Stanley); Instalco's CEO said cost inflation's impact was "very limited" in Q1 2026 and that price clauses and closer supplier relationships learned in 2021 to 2022 are in place; inventory is flat at about SEK 190m.
  • Contract mix is the transmission: 57 percent cost-plus or variable, 43 percent fixed-price (46 percent in 2021), so just under half the book carries input risk between signing and completion, and large fixed-price projects (SEK 25m to 75m) are the hardest to adjust (ABG). This is why wage inflation matters more than metals: labour is 36 percent of sales and repriced annually; metals are a fraction of the 40 percent materials line and partly indexed.
  • Supplier concentration: Ahlsell holds about 30 percent of Swedish installation distribution (21 percent Nordic, 2.3x its next competitor); Sonepar, Rexel and Onninen control 82 percent of Swedish and 91 percent of Finnish electrical distribution; Ahlsell bought Rexel's Finnish business in 2025. Instalco buys about 30 percent centrally and 70 percent locally, direct to site with no inventory. The structure hands pricing power upstream in inflation spikes; it is a known, stable fact, not a change.
  • Availability: skilled technicians are the binding constraint across the Nordics (Danish technical unemployment 1 to 2 percent; Sweco, Sparebanken Møre). High-voltage cable and transformer lead times (138 weeks for high-voltage transformers) delay infrastructure projects but mainly for grid contractors, not Instalco's building work.
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C68

Judgement: turned, about three quarters into recovery, early in the cycle. The organic engine has restarted; the acquired engine was switched off in 2025 to H1 2026 and is being restarted by one large step (Fabri) rather than by the historical bolt-on flow. The turn is real on volume, unproven on price and on cash.

  • The organic series: FY2023 +4.6 percent organic, FY2024 -6.5 percent, FY2025 -0.3 percent (the Change Forces digest states -1.0 percent against the 10 percent target; the two figures are the annual report's and the broker's bases and both are negative), then Q4 2025 +4.9 or +5.0 percent, Q1 2026 +4.9 percent, Q2 2026 +8.4 percent against a 4.1 percent consensus. Sweden organic reached about 9 percent in Q2 2026, Norway 4.9 percent, Finland 30.6 percent in Q1 2026 on industrial projects (many physically in Sweden).
  • The acquired series: 14.3 percent in FY2023, 0.7 percent in FY2025, 1.1 percent in Q2 2026; acquisition spend SEK 397m in FY2024, SEK 316m in FY2025, SEK 56m in H1 2026 against a historical SEK 800m a year. Fabri (51 percent from 1 August 2026) adds EUR 86m of 2025 sales (EUR 117m pro forma, roughly SEK 1.3bn annualised) at a 12.7 percent EBITA margin, with a one-month reporting lag, so FY2026 carries about four to five months of Fabri and Q3 2026 at most two.
  • Where in the cycle: the recovery began in late 2025 (Instalco: the market "bottomed out in late 2025"); volumes are about 15 percent below normal (DNB Carnegie); the backlog ratio moved from 1.07x (Q3 2025) to 1.10x (Q4 2025) to 1.2x (Q1 and Q2 2026). On the research's own lag, installation revenue follows starts by two to four quarters, and starts turned in mid-2026, so the installation revenue effect of the broad turn arrives from H1 2027. That is consistent with the sector digest: upgrades land in 2027 and 2028.
  • What is not turning: price (C-17), cash (operating cash flow SEK 104m in Q2 2026 against SEK 202m) and the bolt-on engine. The turn in the next six months will therefore show in revenue and EBITA before it shows in cash and before it shows in acquired growth outside Germany.
C-07

Macro force: acting at which level, and where does it bite hardest?

❌ D36

Question asked To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

Judgement: the macro force that matters is the discount rate, and it bites hardest at the company level (its leverage and its financing cost) and at the valuation level, before it bites demand. At sector level Sweden's rate sensitivity is a second-order drag on the 2027 recovery; at industry level the force is neutral.

  • The regime (the weekly Fitness for Fighting digest, 16 October 2026): a late-cycle rates squeeze turned into a discount-rate shock, the US 10-year at about 5.1 percent, the first credit crack inside the artificial-intelligence build-out (CoreWeave bonds below 78), European breadth in the bottom quarter, Sweden moved from Overweight to Neutral because the Riksbank "materially strengthened its tightening signal", Fragility Double Underweight, and "the market refused leverage". The digest's question for any case is "who funds the next three years of spending?" and Instalco's answer is: a SEK 3.4bn bank facility (SEK 2.8bn drawn, SEK 1,050m headroom), at 2.6x leverage, with a margin step-up grid tied to leverage.
  • Company level: net financial items absorbed 14 to 20 percent of EBIT through 2025 and 2026 (DNB Carnegie); net interest expense was SEK 167m in FY2025 and Q2 2026's SEK 63m annualises above SEK 240m; the debt is bank debt in local currencies with a leverage-based margin step-up; nothing in the files says the rate is fixed. Each 100 basis points on about SEK 3.5bn of gross debt is about SEK 35m before tax, about SEK 0.10 of EPS, about 4 percent of FY2026 consensus EPS. Consensus has net financial items falling from about SEK 234m in FY2026 to about SEK 133m in FY2028 (pre-tax profit less EBIT), which requires both deleveraging and no rate rise; the Riksbank path argues the second half of that is at risk.
  • Sector level: Sweden is among the most rate-sensitive developed economies because mortgages reset; real estate borrowing costs rose from 1.8 percent (2021) to 3.8 percent (2023), eased to 3.4 percent by mid-2026 and are expected back at 4.0 percent by 2028 (DNB Carnegie). This delays the private developer leg of the recovery and leaves the public and industrial leg, which is where Instalco's backlog growth is (Norway +40.9 percent on public infrastructure).
  • Industry level: industrial capital goods are "split rather than moved"; the relevant Industry rule is "in a short-cycle turn the cheap, geared names with costs cut win", which is the frame the market has applied to Instalco (+63 percent in twelve months) and which the regime is now refusing.
  • The skew: a rates-relief path (Brent under $85, US 10-year under 4.5 percent) would help Instalco at both the valuation and the financing line; a harder risk-off path hits it first through the multiple (a levered small cap at USD 2.9m of daily turnover) and then through its customers' credit.
C-08

Industry or sector key driver accelerating the company's demand?

C54

Question asked To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

Judgement: the sector's fastest driver (data-centre and grid work) accelerates Instalco's demand only indirectly, by absorbing competitors' capacity; its direct accelerators are public health, education, prisons and defence in the Nordics, industrial work in Finland, and the German renovation framework through Fabri. These are firm, not fast.

  • The sector's driver: Bravida's order intake +44 percent in Q2 2026 on SEK 4.3bn Green Mountain (Norway), EUR 200m atNorth (Finland) and SEK 650m EcoDataCenter (Sweden); data centres potentially 20 to 30 percent of Bravida's EBITA by 2030; Finnish data-centre starts (Lahti, Oulu) drawing Fasadgruppen into Finland. Instalco's CEO says data-centre cooling and electrical requirements "are absorbing a substantial portion of available installation resources across the Nordic region"; Instalco itself names no data-centre contract. The benefit to Instalco is the one DNB Carnegie names: competitor capacity leaving the mid-sized market.
  • Instalco's own accelerators: the SEK 100m Skövde prison partnering contract (HVAC, plumbing, cooling), the NOK 440m Aker Hospital turnkey contract (Lysteknikk), the Skanska framework for preschools, schools and sports halls 2026 to 2032 (signed 15 October 2026), the VAROPreem refinery scaffolding framework 2026 to 2029 (one of Sweden's largest), the NCC SEK 100m Stockholm renovation subcontract, Finnish industrial piping at high utilisation (Q1 2026 organic +30.6 percent). Technical consulting (Intec) "experienced continuous strengthening" in H1 2026 as customers prioritise early-stage design for smart systems, which is a leading indicator for installation twelve to eighteen months out.
  • The accelerator that is new: Germany. Fabri's backlog was EUR 69m at end-Q1 2026; the German technical services market is EUR 100bn to 120bn with 91,300 installers; the GModG and BEG subsidies apply from 2026. For the model, Fabri is a consolidation story at 12.7 percent margins rather than a demand story; German heat-pump demand shows "extreme short-term regulatory sensitivity" (Jefferies).
C-09

Other material external dynamic, positive or negative?

❌ D48

Question asked What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

Judgement: three dynamics the question groups do not otherwise capture, two negative and one two-sided. (1) Unhedged currency with a strengthening krona consensus is a modest, persistent translation headwind. (2) The artificial-intelligence financing crack threatens the data-centre tailwind that is currently propping Nordic installer pricing, second-order for Instalco but first-order for the sector narrative it has re-rated with. (3) Germany's market structure is both a long runway and a bidding contest with private equity.

  • Currency: policy is not to hedge; 71 percent of sales are SEK, 17 percent NOK, 12 percent EUR, costs match revenues by currency so transaction risk is immaterial, but translation cost SEK 149m of sales (-1.1 percent) in FY2025 and SEK -158m of equity reserves; a 10 percent NOK move is SEK 48m of translation reserve, a 10 percent EUR move SEK 35m; a 5 percent NOK move is about SEK 115m of sales; a severe 20 percent NOK and EUR depreciation is SEK 0.14 of EPS. Morgan Stanley targets EUR/SEK toward 10.40 and NOK/SEK toward 0.98; Deutsche Bank calls SEK significantly undervalued. Direction: a stronger krona lowers reported NOK and EUR earnings, and from August 2026 Fabri adds EUR exposure. Model it as a 1 to 2 percent annual drag on reported growth, not as a thesis item.
  • The financing crack: the weekly digest's lesson is that "a capital-intensive theme cracks at its financing before its demand". Nordic co-location developers (Green Mountain, atNorth, EcoDataCenter) are externally financed; if their funding tightens, Bravida's capacity returns to the general market and the pricing relief Instalco is enjoying (C-02) reverses. Northvolt (SEK 64m written off by Instalco in Q1 2025, SEK 186m of provisions at Bravida) is the Nordic precedent for a credit event reaching installer receivables.
  • Germany: Brookfield has bought 15 German technical installers since 2023, Nimlas is entering Germany and Poland, SPIE bought ROFA and SGS (EUR 610m of revenue) in 2026, mid-sized German technical deals go at 7 to 9x EBITA (Bernstein) against Instalco's Nordic 3 to 6x. Fabri's platform (23 subsidiaries) is a seat at the table; the arbitrage is thinner than at home.
  • Smaller items: Bravida's over-invoicing case (Region Skåne; a 10 percent one-day share fall; convictions in late 2025) is a standing read-across risk to every decentralised Nordic installer with public clients; Swedish municipalities audited 30 Bravida customers and found nothing more, which bounds it.
C-10

Each thematic driver in turn, then all together: headwinds and tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and the overall external picture.

C57

Question asked Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Judgement: the external picture is moderately positive, cyclical in nature, with visibility inside two years and the main risks sitting at the financing and pricing links rather than at demand. Model it as a recovery with a public-sector floor and a rate-set ceiling.

Driver by driver:

  • Nordic construction recovery (sector thematic): tailwind, strong on volume, early, fading after the easy comparatives; visibility 12 to 18 months. Strength: 7 of 10.
  • Renovation and energy-efficiency regulation: tailwind, structural, slow; lengthens the tail into 2028 to 2033; does not move 2027. Strength: 5 of 10 near term, 7 of 10 long term.
  • Data centres, electrification and grids: tailwind for the sector, indirect for Instalco (competitor capacity absorbed, pricing relief); fragile to the artificial-intelligence financing cycle. Strength for Instalco: 4 of 10.
  • Public health, education, prisons and defence: tailwind, firm, dated budgets; Instalco's named wins sit here. Strength: 6 of 10.
  • Labour scarcity: two-sided; it is the industry's price floor (prevents price wars) and its volume cap (stalls organic growth below 2 percent for the scaled player that cannot hire); for Instalco the 2027 wage round turns it into a 2027 cost. Net: neutral to slight headwind.
  • Interest rates and the discount rate: headwind, the most important one; bites financing cost, the multiple and Swedish private demand, in that order. Strength: 7 of 10 against.
  • Competitive intensity: easing from a peak but elevated in Instalco's mid-sized tender band; fragile. Slight headwind.
  • Input costs and suppliers: materials neutral, wholesalers absorbing, labour a 2027 headwind; structural supplier concentration. Slight headwind.
  • Currency: modest translation headwind.

By level:

  • Value chain: customers (construction companies 48 percent of sales) are price-led and credit-risky but ordering again; wholesalers are absorbing inflation (tailwind); subcontractors available; labour scarce. Net slightly positive now, turning neutral in 2027.
  • Industry (Nordic technical installation): fragmented, late-cyclical, now in year one of recovery with volumes 15 percent below normal; margins rise with utilisation rather than price. Positive.
  • Sector (industrial services in Europe): split; the installers that pruned for margin have re-rated and now need earnings to arrive; the regime is refusing leverage and re-ratings. Neutral for the business, negative for the stock.

Together: tailwinds outweigh headwinds on the operating lines for the next 12 months, with the honest caveat that the strongest tailwind is comparatives-and-utilisation rather than a new source of demand, and that the single strongest headwind (rates) acts on the lines below EBITA and on the price.

Internal change forces

C6413 questions
C-1176C-1270C-1344C-1466C-1552C-1662C-1740C-1850C-1940C-2055C-8640C-8755C-2166

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The internal change is the stronger half of the case and the half the Consumer can model with the most confidence: a founder with 8.56 percent of the equity, a clear and repeated priority (margin), a rebuilt second tier, standards that are visible in fewer write-downs and in a 120 basis point margin step within one quarter, and a well-structured entry into a market five times the size of the home one. Its limits are that nothing has been raised (targets kept, dividend cut, no bridge, no dates), that the acquisition engine which made this company is off and restarts only when cash and leverage allow, that 49 percent of the German profit leaks to minorities for years, and that the method (baseline plus autonomy) carries a cultural risk every research family names. Model the margin recovery as the company's own 2017 to 2021 history re-run under the same man, at a slower pace than his urgency implies (the sell-side's own Swedish path is 7.2 percent in 2027 and 7.6 percent in 2028, under the 8 percent group target), and model the growth engine as off until leverage prints below 2.5x.

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, against the last 12 to 18 months.

C-11

Change in the chief executive, key leaders, board or key actors, and what it signals.

B76

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement: a complete change at the top, of the rare kind where the founder returns to run what he built, with a full second tier rebuilt beneath him. It signals that margin, not growth, is the organisation's objective for the next two years, and that the prior era's hands-off decentralisation has been replaced by decentralisation with accountability. The signal is strong; its two qualifications are the founder's divided time and the fact that he was chairman while the deterioration happened.

  • The sequence: Robin Boheman stepped down as CEO on 31 July 2025 after margin erosion and leverage reaching 3.1x; founder Per Sjöstrand (CEO 2014 to 2021, then chairman) became acting CEO on 1 August 2025 and permanent President and CEO on 19 March 2026, the board abandoning an external search; Johnny Alvarsson (CEO of Beijer Alma) became chairman on 31 July 2025 and was retained at the May 2026 AGM; the Head of M&A left on 1 July 2025; country managers were appointed on 1 September 2025 (Johan Larsson Sweden, Roger Aksnes Norway, Jukka-Pekka Kujala Finland) with Patrik Persson as Swedish COO; the Extended Management Team was phased out on 1 January 2026; Fabri's CEO Markus Zübert joined group management on the majority step in August 2026. CFO Christina Kassberg (since November 2021, ex-Addtech) stayed.
  • What the actions say: within one quarter of his return the group adjusted EBITA margin went from 6.0 percent (Q3 2025) to 7.2 percent (Q4 2025); the dividend was cut from SEK 0.68 to SEK 0.50; Swedish acquisitions were paused (one in 2025, none in Q1 2026); the organisation was cut from thirteen business areas to three countries; and the founder bought 42,000 shares in the open market in Q4 2025 on top of his 22,999,835 (8.56 percent). He does not participate in warrant programmes. His stated priority on 24 October 2025: "margin, margin and margin"; his commitment is to stay until the 8 percent margin is "sustainably" reached.
  • The qualifications the Consumer should keep in view: he chaired six external boards at the time of his return (Green Landscaping, Eltel, Knowit, Uniwater, Hantverksgruppen, ByggPartnerGruppen), against a Swedish norm of two to three; he declined re-election at Green Landscaping in April 2026 but still chairs Eltel and Knowit, so his executive capacity is a fair question (DNB Carnegie says delegation to Larsson and Persson mitigates it). He was chairman from 2021 through the period in which margins fell from 8.2 percent to 5.9 percent and leverage rose to 3.3x, so his return is a correction of an outcome he oversaw. He was paid SEK 1.94m through a consultancy arrangement in the interim period under a board derogation.
  • No activist, no change in major holders (AMF Pension and Capital Group each 6.5 to 7.9 percent; the ten largest hold 53 percent).
C-12

Change in strategy or strategic priorities, and how well articulated and resourced.

C70

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement: a real and well-articulated change of priority (margin over volume) and of operating method (Instalco 2.0: a baseline of minimum standards, country-level accountability, selective bidding and customer prioritisation, conditional acquisitions), resourced with people rather than money. What is missing is a numerical bridge: the company has stated the destination (8 percent) and the method, not the building blocks or the dates.

  • The choices, in the company's own terms: "margin over volume"; "discipline over volume"; "we are moving a bit from the hands-off approach"; "significant freedom under significant responsibility" with "much tougher operational requirements" for project selection; "the goal is to reduce person dependence and create a shared language around performance". Acquisitions now follow margin improvement, debt reduction and cash flow, a reversal of 2022 to 2023 when the pace continued as cash conversion fell.
  • The method: the "baseline" (standard costing templates, risk assessment and self-assessments completed by every subsidiary in spring 2026); ABC customer categorisation steering order-taking toward repeat public and industrial clients; the Go-Great improvement teams converted from a help-desk into an accountable division and expanded in Norway and Finland; peer mentorship where strong subsidiary CEOs coach weak ones; quarterly internal control reports; country COOs. Management describes it as "pull not push": the best subsidiaries see little change, the weak ones get targeted support.
  • Resourcing: the country structure added COOs and management the market had not modelled; central overhead remains tiny; capital is reserved for deleveraging and the German platform. No cost programme with a number is attached (contrast Bravida's SEK 65m Swedish savings on SEK 90m to 110m of charges), because the plan is behavioural rather than structural.
  • The strategic adjacencies are consistent with the core: Intec (early-stage design, pulling installation work), Inmatiq (automation, now break-even), scaffolding (TSM, April 2026), Germany (same decentralised model, founders locked in to 2033). The one strategic tension is between the baseline and the entrepreneurial culture the model depends on, which every research family names as the largest execution risk.
C-13

Guidance raised, or mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions set or raised?

❌ D44

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement: no. Nothing has been raised; the targets were kept while the dividend was cut. The upward movement in the last six months is entirely the sell-side's (target prices +24 percent in six months, buy share up 14 points to 86 percent) and the Fabri consolidation (about 10 percent added to pro forma EBITDA). For the model this means the company's own ambition is a ceiling that consensus now reaches only in 2028, and there is no company-authored path to test consensus against.

  • The standing targets, unchanged for years: sales growth of at least 10 percent a year over a business cycle; EBITA margin of at least 8 percent; net debt to EBITDA not above 2.5x over a cycle; cash conversion of at least 100 percent; dividend payout of about 30 percent. FY2025 delivery: organic -0.3 to -1.0 percent, reported margin 5.9 percent (adjusted 6.4 percent), leverage 2.8x, cash conversion 108 percent, payout 39 percent of a depressed profit.
  • What changed in the targets' status rather than their level: under Boheman the 8 percent was treated as a through-cycle average; under Sjöstrand it is "a matter of corporate urgency" and the condition for his continued tenure. That is a change in the credibility of the target, not in guidance.
  • No capital markets day is scheduled or mooted in the files; no quarterly or annual guidance is given; the dividend proposal (SEK 0.50 against a SEK 0.68 expectation) was the only numerical signal and it was downward.
  • The gap to consensus (C-68): consensus EBIT implies EBITA margins of about 7.0 percent in FY2026, 7.8 percent in FY2027 and 8.0 percent in FY2028 (EBIT plus about SEK 135m of amortisation over revenue), so the 8 percent is now in the numbers for 2028 and nearly in them for 2027. The broker's June 2026 arithmetic, that reaching 8 percent would lift consensus EBITA by 15 percent, has narrowed to a few percent. The target no longer carries upside; delivery does.
C-14

Standards improving in execution, operations, governance or cost discipline?

C66

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement: yes, demonstrably in execution and credit control, with the evidence in the numbers (fewer project deviations, no growth in old overdue receivables, 100 percent cash conversion in Q1 2026 after 108 percent in FY2025, net working capital at 1.6 percent of sales at end-2025 from a 2.8 percent peak in 2022); not yet in the one standard the market will judge first, which is quarterly cash conversion (90 percent rolling at Q2 2026). Governance standards were already high and unchanged.

  • Execution: management reports "fewer major negative project deviations" than in 2025 thanks to early identification; the Q4 2025 and Q2 2026 margins (7.2 and 7.1 percent) came with no new write-downs; restructuring in late 2024 closed or merged eight loss-making subsidiaries (SEK -30m of EBITA) and removed 110 employees and 100 subcontractors; the Finland segment went from 2.0 percent (Q1 2025) to 12.1 percent (Q1 2026) on utilisation and execution, then 8.1 percent in Q2 2026.
  • Cost discipline and cash: the CFO's finance team moved from tracking broad metrics to tracking specific accounts; credit monitoring was strengthened after Northvolt; working capital was 1.1 percent of sales at Q1 2026 (company basis) before the Q2 build to 2.3 percent on late-quarter invoicing.
  • Governance: six of seven directors independent; audit committee met six times with full attendance; auditor rotation in charge for 2026 (Ida Sparrfeldt succeeding Camilla Nilsson at Grant Thornton); whistleblowing externally managed; first peer to do a double materiality assessment under CSRD; zero code deviations in 2025. No change, because none was needed.
  • Where standards have not visibly improved: employee turnover rose to 15.8 percent in 2025 from 13.1 percent in 2023 (Bravida 20.4 percent); eNPS flat at 31; occupational injury frequency 17.0 per million hours flagged by the broker; the recurrence of "non-recurring" items (SEK 65m Q4 2024, SEK 64m Q1 2025, SEK 11m Q2 2025, SEK 75m FY2025) is a reporting-discipline point the Consumer should keep separate from operating discipline.
C-15

Reducing perimeter or composition: divestments, simplification, exits from low-return units?

C52

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement: modest simplification, no divestment, and a net widening of the perimeter. The actions taken are the right ones for margin (closing loss-makers, collapsing thirteen business areas to three countries, removing a management layer); they are small in money and are more than offset by Germany, scaffolding and automation.

  • Actions: eight underperforming subsidiaries closed or merged in late 2024 (SEK 65m of restructuring cost, SEK 29m of goodwill impairment, SEK -30m of EBITA removed), 110 FTEs and 100 subcontractors terminated, the two largest Norwegian subsidiaries downsized by 30 percent in 2021 (precedent), the Extended Management Team phased out on 1 January 2026, the Inmatiq margin drag (about 30 basis points on Sweden in late 2024) brought to break-even by Q1 2026. "No material divestitures or asset sales in the last 12 months" (Leadership digest, confirmed absent).
  • Composition: the perimeter grew by one country (Germany, 23 subsidiaries), one discipline (industrial scaffolding, TSM Taksäkerhetsmontörerna 70 percent in April 2026, plus the VAROPreem framework) and one adjacency (building automation). The subsidiary count stays around 150 in the Nordics.
  • For predictability (C-79): the simplification is at the management layer, where it helps; the operating perimeter, which is where projects get mispriced, is wider, not narrower.
C-16

Entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly?

C62

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement: yes, materially, and sensibly structured: Germany through a staged majority in a local platform with founders locked in to 2033, and two organic adjacencies (consulting, automation) that feed the core. The sense of it is in the structure; the risk of it is distance (Germany shares the model, not the customers or channels) and leakage (49 percent minorities and undisclosed option formulas).

  • Germany: Fabri AG, 24 percent for EUR 15m in March 2025 (funded by a SEK 138m targeted share issue), 51 percent from 1 August 2026 for EUR 35.7m (funded from the credit facility), a further 17 percent in 2029 at the earliest conditional on earnings hurdles, options on the remaining 32 percent in 2030 to 2033; 2025 sales EUR 86m (EUR 117m pro forma including 2025 acquisitions), EBITA margin 12.7 percent, backlog EUR 69m at end-Q1 2026, 12 subsidiaries at entry and 23 now; the initial stake was bought at under 10x EBITA (DNB Carnegie) against German mid-sized deals at 7 to 9x (Bernstein). The German market is EUR 100bn to 120bn, five times the Nordics, with 91,300 firms and the top five under 15 percent; 51 percent of targets sit in Bavaria, Baden-Württemberg and North Rhine-Westphalia. Barriers for a foreign entrant (language, rigid labour regulation, relationship-dependent deal flow) are exactly why a local platform is the sensible route (expert calls, Ramboll and Indra). Fabri keeps its brands and decentralised decisions; Instalco is explicitly importing a business model, not customers.
  • The leakage the Consumer must model: 49 percent of Fabri's earnings go to minorities until the option steps; the EBITA performance hurdles for step 3 and the valuation formula for step 4 are undisclosed (low confidence, key-question research); contingent consideration by segment is not disclosed; the broker flags the Fabri option as a potential cash drag in late 2026. DNB Carnegie's judgement that Fabri is "broadly leverage-neutral" (net debt and EBITDA both up about 10 percent) is a consolidation-arithmetic point, not a cash point.
  • Product adjacencies: Intec (technical consulting, about 500 employees across 40 locations since 2020, margins above group, works with external competitors too) is the proven template; Inmatiq (building automation, May 2024, Siemens and Schneider partnerships, SEK 30m Region Blekinge framework, break-even Q1 2026) is the copy; scaffolding is a bolt-on. Against the "core versus adjacency" test, consulting and automation draw on the core's customers and channels; Germany draws only on its skills.
  • Sensibility verdict: the German structure is the best-designed thing in the file (staged, hurdle-conditioned, founder-locked, local CEO in group management). Its sense will be tested by whether German bolt-ons can be bought at the Nordic 3 to 6x or only at the German 7 to 9x, with private equity (Brookfield, 15 deals since 2023) in the same pool.
C-17

Raising prices in real terms: real, nominal, or latent?

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement: latent at best. There is no evidence of price rises above cost inflation; there is evidence of price discipline (declining low-margin work) and of pass-through clauses working on materials, and there is a stated intent (value-added pricing training, ABC categorisation). The company cannot itself separate price from volume across 6,000 projects, and neither can the Consumer; model zero real price.

  • What is evidenced: the 2021 to 2022 lesson (index clauses, closer supplier relationships) means material inflation is now passed through in 57 percent of the book; the CEO reported "very limited" cost-inflation impact in Q1 2026; a growing pool of metropolitan projects "allowed local subsidiary managers to become far more selective in their bidding"; DNB Carnegie observes a "step-up in the margin quality of the group's order backlog".
  • What is not evidenced: any increase in realised prices on like-for-like work; the research states the price-to-volume split of the 8.4 percent organic growth "remains unquantified" and suggests tracking distributor index reports; sector-wide, 40 percent of contractors are pushing back on price hikes, public buyers prioritise lowest price, 15 bidders compete. The ABC programme's margin uplift "is not reported on a standalone basis".
  • The historical test is unkind: adjusted EBITA margin fell from 8.2 percent in 2021 to 5.9 percent in 2025 through the inflation period, so whatever pricing power exists did not hold margin through the last cycle; it was volume loss and legacy fixed-price work, not price, that explained the fall, which is the same thing said differently: the company could not reprice.
  • Where real price is plausible: service (35 percent of sales, not contractually bound, repeat customers), partnering contracts with transparent cost-plus, and the Finnish industrial niche at high utilisation. These are the lines where the model may carry 1 to 2 percent of price; the tender book should carry none until the Q3 or Q4 2026 reports show Swedish margin above 7.5 percent without volume explaining it.
C-18

Stepping up mergers and acquisitions; how sensible, disciplined and well integrated?

C50

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement: the opposite of a step-up: the acquisition engine was deliberately stopped (SEK 56m of consideration in H1 2026 against a historical SEK 800m a year and SEK 1.1bn to 1.7bn of acquired annual sales in the peak years 2021 to 2023) and one large, debt-funded step was taken instead (Fabri). The discipline of the pause is a point in management's favour; the engine's restart is the sell-side's assumption for 2027 and is gated on leverage below 2.5x, which depends on the cash recovery. Integration is by design minimal (brands and P&L kept; three-year lock-ups), so integration risk sits at lock-up expiry, not at closing.

  • Discipline, historical: more than 150 transactions since 2014 at 3 to 6x EV/EBITA, targets required to show stable margins above 5 percent and high customer retention; 27 deals adding SEK 1,760m of annual sales in 2021 alone. The weakness in the record is funding: unlike Lifco and Addtech, Instalco has issued shares to fund pace (the research's point), and the 2022 to 2023 pace continued while cash conversion fell and leverage reached 3.3x.
  • The pause: zero acquisitions in Q1 2026; TSM scaffolding (70 percent) in April 2026; acquisition cash SEK 397m in FY2024, SEK 316m in FY2025, SEK 56m in H1 2026; the Head of M&A left in July 2025. Management's stated sequence: margin, debt reduction, cash flow, then acquisitions.
  • The restart arithmetic (DNB Carnegie, February 2026): a conservative SEK 300m of acquired sales a year at a 7 percent Nordic margin adds about SEK 21m of EBITA; the base SEK 800m adds about SEK 56m and lifts group margin toward 7.8 percent by 2028; an accelerated SEK 1.2bn adds about SEK 84m and reaches 8.0 percent; base-case EPS growth of 15 percent in 2027 and 12 percent in 2028 "compared to flat organic profiles". The restart is "implied late 2026 based on leverage trajectory, but no explicit management commitment disclosed" (medium confidence). Valuation precedent: Nordic serial acquirers trade at 13 to 14.5x EV/EBITA with the engine running and 10.2 to 12.2x with it frozen.
  • Integration and the lock-up cliff: subsidiaries keep brands and full P&L, so there is no integration programme to fail; the risk is entrepreneur flight at 36 months (expert calls; the risks digest's named leading indicator) and the friction of the baseline with autonomy. No subsidiary CEO departures are named in the files; employee turnover of 15.8 percent is the proxy.
  • Germany as the strategic restart: Fabri's option path (17 percent in 2029, 32 percent in 2030 to 2033) is a committed future use of capital at undisclosed prices; German bolt-ons at 7 to 9x and private-equity competition thin the arbitrage; the earnings review asks whether Instalco has the bandwidth to scale Fabri from 23 to "50-plus" subsidiaries by 2029 without repeating the Swedish write-down mistakes.
C-19

Making more use of the balance sheet, or stepping up shareholder returns, or mooting either?

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement: the balance sheet is being used for one thing, the German step, and otherwise repaired; shareholder returns were cut, not raised; buybacks are neither used nor mooted. For the next 18 months the only plausible "increase in use" is the acquisition restart, and management has made it conditional.

  • Use: Fabri step 2 (EUR 35.7m) from the SEK 3.4bn facility; the facility's one-year extension exercised on 29 June 2026 (runs to at least 2028; Danske Bank, SEB, Svensk Exportkredit); total facilities SEK 3,850m, SEK 2,800m drawn, SEK 1,050m of headroom including cash; covenants on net debt to EBITDA and interest cover "met with a wide margin" and a leverage-based margin step-up.
  • Returns: dividend cut to SEK 0.50 (39 percent payout) from SEK 0.68, explicitly to accelerate deleveraging; consensus dividends SEK 0.70, 0.90, 0.90 for FY2026 to FY2028; the AGM's 5 percent buyback mandate was not used in 2025 and there is no mention of using it; warrant programmes (2.35m warrants 2023/2026 at SEK 64.90, 2.35m 2024/2027 at SEK 44.32, up to 1.95m 2026/2029) are the only share-count item and are a small dilution (0.7 to 0.9 percent each) rather than a return.
  • Mooting: nothing on buybacks or specials; the only forward use mooted is M&A once leverage is below 2.5x. Contrast Bravida (two SEK 100m buybacks in 2026 at 1.3x leverage).
C-20

Other material positive corporate change forces present or plausible?

C55

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Judgement: four smaller forces, each worth a line in the model's assumptions rather than a driver: the automation and consulting start-ups turning from drag to contributor; frameworks that lengthen visibility; the Finnish industrial niche; and an internal artificial-intelligence platform the CEO rates highly but which has no number attached.

  • Inmatiq: first quarter without an earnings drag in Q1 2026; Siemens Smart Infrastructure top-tier partnership; the Blekinge framework (two years plus two, about SEK 30m). The earnings review's "outlier call" is 50 to 100 basis points of group margin by 2028 if it scales like Intec; the sell-side treats it as immaterial. Carry as upside, not base.
  • Intec: about 500 employees, margins above group, "faster than management originally expected", pulls downstream installation leads; consulting demand strengthening through H1 2026.
  • Frameworks: Skanska 2026 to 2032 (preschools, schools, sports halls), VAROPreem 2026 to 2029 (refinery scaffolding), Region Blekinge; these extend the 12 to 18 month visibility on a small share of sales.
  • Finland: industrial piping at high utilisation produced a 12.1 percent segment margin in Q1 2026; management warns it will fluctuate (8.1 percent in Q2); from Q3 2026 Finland and Germany are one segment, so the Fabri margin will blend with it.
  • The internal artificial-intelligence platform: the CEO calls it "one of the most powerful efficiency drivers" he has seen in 45 years; no cost or saving is quantified. Treat as zero until it is.
  • Precedent at the founder's other platforms: Green Landscaping (26 percent revenue CAGR 2019 to 2024 at 4 to 6x EV/EBITA) and Eltel (eleven consecutive quarters of margin improvement to Q1 2026) show the same person running the same playbook elsewhere with results; this is a soft positive on the probability that Instalco 2.0 delivers.
C-86

Negative corporate change developments present or plausible?

❌ D40

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement: five, of which two are live in the numbers (cash conversion and the Finnish fade), two are structural (minority leakage and culture friction) and one is reputational (concentration of research coverage and the founder's time). None is yet a thesis-breaker; together they are the shape of the case against.

  • Cash: operating cash flow SEK 104m in Q2 2026 against SEK 202m; rolling cash conversion 90 percent (107, 112, 108, 100, 90 over five quarters); receivables SEK 2,009m (a record), contract assets SEK 808m from SEK 551m at Q4 2025; the key-question analysis's Analysis of Competing Hypotheses gives the structural-consumption and quality hypotheses two hard inconsistencies each against three for pure timing, concluding a composite of Swedish timing and Norwegian structural consumption (long public projects). The company's defence: overdue receivables did not increase; Q4 2025 released SEK 451m. The Q3 print decides this (threshold: operating cash flow recovering toward SEK 200m; receivables below SEK 1,800m would vindicate timing).
  • Finland: 12.1 percent (Q1 2026) to 8.1 percent (Q2) against 9.9 percent consensus; the broker will not extrapolate the H2 2025 level; from Q3 the segment merges with Germany, which both blends and obscures it.
  • Minority leakage and option liabilities: 49 percent of Fabri to minorities; step 3 and 4 formulas undisclosed; call and put options on subsidiary shares are a known feature of these roll-ups (Fasadgruppen carries SEK 713m of such options); Instalco's own figure is not in the files, a data gap.
  • Culture: turnover 15.8 percent; lock-ups expiring at 36 months; the baseline and country COOs are "a notable cultural tightening"; customers sometimes prefer unified brands to federations. The Leadership digest's own "aggregate weak signals" cascade: cultural friction, overboarding, brand fragmentation, sell-side scepticism on timing, out-of-the-money warrants (the 2023/2026 series at SEK 64.90 is far out of the money) losing retention value.
  • Coverage and time: three brokers, one of them (DNB Carnegie) dominant, Nordea at Hold since 19 October 2026; the founder's external chairmanships; a SEK 1.94m consultancy arrangement. A Bravida-type over-invoicing allegation at any Nordic installer would be read across.
  • Plausible but not present: a dilutive raise if write-downs and weak cash push leverage toward covenants (the research's consistent "other side"); a Swedish margin reversal when Q1 2027 comparatives harden.
C-87

Key controlling actors indicating scope for change in strategy or approach?

C55

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement: no. The controlling actor is the founder himself, and the change he indicated is the one now being executed. Institutional holders are passive, there is no activist, and the register is stable.

  • Founder 8.56 percent (22,999,835 shares), the largest holder, sole non-independent director, buying in Q4 2025; AMF Pension 6.5 to 7.8 percent and Capital Group 6.5 to 7.9 percent; the ten largest 53.2 percent (Bravida's register is fragmented by contrast); Cliens and AMF on the nomination committee; "no active activist presence"; credit facilities require renegotiation if any outside holder passes 50 percent, a defensive clause.
  • The board re-elected all seven directors in May 2026 and backed the founder's permanent appointment; the only strategic shift any actor has signalled is his (margin first, Germany, conditional M&A).
  • What this means for the model: no event-driven change (break-up, sale, recapitalisation) should be carried; equally, no outside check on the founder's choices should be assumed.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C66

Judgement: strong and broad on the margin-recovery axis, where five internal forces act at once (founder-CEO, strategy change, standards, perimeter simplification, new geography) and the outputs have followed on three of four lines (revenue growth, margin, leverage); narrow and unresourced on the growth-engine axis, where the bolt-on flow is stopped and its restart is conditional on the one output that has not followed (cash). The change is best described as a restoration with new structures, not a transformation: the destination (8 percent, 10 percent growth, 2.5x) is the company's 2017 to 2021 self.

  • The strength is visible: adjusted EBITA margin 6.0 percent (Q3 2025) to 7.2 percent (Q4 2025) to 7.1 percent (Q2 2026); leverage 3.3x to 2.6x in three quarters; organic growth from -3.6 percent to +8.4 percent; a Swedish segment at 7.0 percent in Q2 2026 from 5.0 percent in Q1 (seasonal) and 6.6 percent a year earlier. The founder's record elsewhere (Green Landscaping, Eltel) and his 8.56 percent stake are the resourcing.
  • The breadth has a hole: nothing in the change set addresses pricing power, the digital gap to Bravida, or the funding model; the German platform addresses the runway, not the engine's fuel.
  • The timing: most of the restoration's first leg (6 percent to 7 percent) is delivered; the second leg (7 percent to 8 percent) requires Swedish legacy contracts to roll off, bad debt to normalise, utilisation to rise 5 percent on revenue per employee, and the 2027 wage round not to intervene. The third leg (growth from M&A at 13 to 14x on 3 to 6x purchases) requires leverage below 2.5x, so it requires the second leg and the cash.
