Industry and Value Chain

❌ D4916 questions
C-2444C-2534C-2650C-2752C-2840C-2942C-3148C-8456C-3258C-3347C-3450C-3550C-3666C-3757C-3850C-3946

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The industry is a fragmented, local, labour-barred, asset-light service with pro-cyclical pricing: it rewards consolidators that keep bid discipline through the trough and have capital to buy at 3 to 6x when the tail is distressed, and it punishes those that chase volume or buy growth with debt. Instalco sits third in Sweden and fourth in the Nordics with a higher margin than Bravida and a lower one than Assemblin Caverion; it has the discipline and the labour culture, and it is short of capital until leverage prints below 2.5x, so for the next two to three quarters it is a price-taker in a market whose pricing is improving on volume. The structure supports the margin recovery (utilisation on a pruned base) and does not support a price-led recovery; it supports a medium-term consolidation story and makes the next year of that story someone else's (Bravida's, Nimlas's, SPIE's). Model: no price, utilisation-driven margin, acquired growth from Fabri only until 2027, and a sector base rate that says the write-down risk is a real tail rather than a remote one.

C-24

Market share or size rank, and distance from number one or two.

❌ D44

Question asked Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

Judgement: fourth in the Nordics at about 4 percent, third in Sweden at about 7 percent (Bravida 12 percent, Assemblin 8 percent, now Assemblin Caverion at about SEK 41bn of combined revenue), marginal in Norway (about 2 percent) and Finland (about 1 percent), and negligible in Germany. Rank confers little in this industry because the market is local; what matters is that Instalco is a top-three player only in the market that produces 71 percent of its sales, and that its two larger rivals are each two to three times its size.

  • Scale: Instalco SEK 13,598m of FY2025 revenue; Bravida SEK 28,208m; Assemblin Caverion about SEK 41bn pro forma (number one in Finland, Sweden and Norway after the April 2024 merger); SPIE EUR 10,380m in Europe. Nordic market about SEK 209bn (Sweden SEK 141bn per Navet), so Instalco's Nordic share is about 6.5 percent on revenue (the broker's 4 percent figure dates from its 2022 initiation and a larger market definition; both say "fourth").
  • Distance to number two in Sweden is one share point; distance to number one is five. In Norway and Finland the gap is an order of magnitude; local leaders are "extremely difficult to dethrone" because scale attracts the scarce technicians (Caverion strategy expert).
  • Germany: Fabri at EUR 117m pro forma in a EUR 100bn to 120bn market is a rounding error; SPIE's 3 percent is the leading pure-play share.
C-25

Market concentration: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the distribution?

❌ D34

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement: no. The top three hold about 23 percent of the Nordic market and the top five an estimated 18 percent (the Bravida research's figure, against 39 percent in France); the long tail of about 25,000 firms holds about 77 percent. The distribution is steep at the very top (Bravida about 8.5 percent, then Assemblin Caverion, then Instalco) and then flat for thousands of firms, which is the structure that both makes consolidation possible and makes pricing discipline impossible to enforce.

  • Country detail: small local actors hold over 65 percent of Sweden, over 80 percent of Norway and over 75 percent of Finland; Germany's top five hold under 15 percent and the four largest 9 percent across 91,300 firms; France by contrast has top-five concentration near 45 percent, which is where the installation stage earns stable margins (SPIE 7.6 percent).
  • Consequence for the model: pricing follows the tail's utilisation, not the leaders' discipline; a 30-percent-plus top five would be a different industry.
C-26

Stability of shares, and new entrants' share over three to five years.

C50

Question asked How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

Judgement: shares are stable at the top and drifting slowly toward consolidators as a class; the "new entrants" of the last three to five years are private-equity-backed roll-ups that have taken share by buying it (Nimlas over 140 companies and SEK 10bn of revenue; InstallatørGruppen 48 deals since 2023, now listed in Copenhagen; Francks Kylindustri 31 deals; Assemblin Caverion by merger), not by winning tenders. Organic share movement between the large players is small and reverses with the cycle: Bravida's Nordic share fell from 9.0 to 8.5 percent in 2025 while Instalco posted 30 percent organic growth in Finland.

  • The research is explicit that barriers to entry are low for a small firm and barriers to scale are high (relationships, certifications, labour), so new entry happens at the bottom of the distribution continuously and at the top only by acquisition.
  • For Instalco the relevant share movement is in Sweden in 2025, where private peers out-earned it on margin while it shrank; share is not disclosed at country level by year in the files.
C-27

Does share consolidate toward the largest and the most advantaged, and is this company among them?

C52

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement: yes, slowly, and the advantaged are not the largest but those with three things at once: access to skilled labour, capital to buy at 3 to 6x while trading at 11 to 14x, and bid discipline through the downturn. Instalco has the first and third and is short of the second until leverage falls below 2.5x; it is "among them" on record (150 deals, 8 percent margins 2017 to 2021) and conditionally on capacity.

  • Consolidation has run since about 2010 and accelerates in distress: 8.5 percent of Nordic firms in financial distress, construction bankruptcies four of every five kroner lost in northern Norway (Sparebanken Nord-Norge), tenders attracting fifteen bidders are the symptom of a tail about to sell.
  • The advantaged set now: Bravida (1.3x leverage, SEK 2.5bn of undrawn facilities, but a data-centre pivot that uses its capital), Assemblin Caverion (Triton-owned, 3.3x leverage, integrating), Nimlas and InstallatørGruppen (private equity, "operate under higher leverage thresholds"), SPIE in Germany (self-funded at 1.3x to 2.1x). Instalco at 2.6x with SEK 1,050m of headroom is the least able of the listed set to buy in 2026; the sector digest's second-order case is precisely "the geared laggards if their balance sheets hold".
  • The company's own labour advantage is real but unmeasured: eNPS 31 against Bravida's 14; turnover 15.8 percent against 20.4 percent.
C-28

Competitors' attitude: margin first or volume first; benign or willing to suffer losses; do prices fall when volume falls?

❌ D40

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement: bifurcated. The listed and private-equity leaders profess margin over volume and mostly practise it (Bravida sacrificed 5 percent of volume in 2025 to hold a 5.9 percent margin; Instalco closed subsidiaries; Assemblin Caverion "prioritises profitability over top-line volume"); the tail of 25,000 firms is volume-first by necessity and bids at zero margin when idle. Industry prices do fall when volume falls, as 2024 to 2025 showed, because the tail sets the tender price and even the leaders "compromise" at the trough.

  • Evidence of the leaders' discipline: Bravida's CEO, "the worst thing you can do is actually to sell low margin projects"; Instalco's "we also choose discipline over volume"; Fasadgruppen now "rejecting low-margin contracts". Evidence of its limits: Bravida "admitted to compromising on this strategy due to intense competitive bidding" (DNB Carnegie); low-bid legacy projects signed in 2024 limit Bravida Sweden's margin in 2026 and Instalco Sweden's in H1 2026.
  • The tail's behaviour: "smaller players face utilisation pressure as volumes fade, triggering highly competitive and irrational pricing in fragmented local markets" (Deutsche Bank); "desperate small competitors submit zero-margin bids during downturns, permanently capping industry-wide margin expansion" (Bravida research).
  • Why it matters: the industry's best-in-class full-cycle EBITA ceiling is 6 to 8.5 percent (Bravida research), with small local firms at 7 to 14 percent EBIT in good years; the ceiling is set by the tail's willingness to suffer.
C-29

Stability of that attitude over 18 months to three years; "Michael O'Leary" instability and "shark attacks".

❌ D42

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement: the leaders' attitude is stable and rational across the cycle; the tail's is situation-dependent and reactive, and it launched a shark attack on Swedish tender pricing from mid-2024 through 2025 as residential volumes collapsed and newbuild contractors pivoted into renovation and public work. That attack is receding with volume in 2026 and would resume if the recovery stalls into the Riksbank's hikes. The instability is therefore cyclical and predictable in its trigger, not in its timing.

  • The attack, dated: specialised-construction pricing sentiment declined from May 2022 to August 2025 (Konjunkturinstitutet); fifteen bidders against five; Balco and Fasadgruppen report newbuild competitors "temporarily shifting into renovation" with intense price pressure in Sweden; Instalco Sweden's margin troughed at 4.1 percent in Q1 2025.
  • The receding: Instalco Q2 2026, "pricing environment stabilised"; Bravida Q2 2026 organic +9 percent at a flat 5.9 percent margin (volume without price); distress and exit in the tail.
  • The precedent that the attack can come from the top too: Assemblin Caverion's merger "did not ease aggressive bidding"; Nimlas's growth plan is volume-led; Bravida's three Swedish divisions merged into one with SEK 65m of savings to be redeployed. A large player that chooses volume to fill a cost base cut by 4 percent of headcount is the shark the Consumer should watch.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

❌ D48

Judgement: moderately supportive to a disciplined consolidator with capital, and fragile because the support is pro-cyclical. The industry gives its participants a labour barrier (licensed technicians, apprentice pipelines), local relational lock-in, an asset-light capital profile, a long consolidation runway at low purchase multiples and a regulatory demand floor; it denies them pricing power at the tender stage, concentration among customers' alternatives, and protection from the tail's behaviour in a downturn. Exit barriers are low (hand tools, vans) but exit is slow (owner-managers persist at a loss), so competitive intensity, which is entry minus exit, rises exactly when volumes fall.

  • The value-chain margin map puts installation at 15 to 25 percent gross and 5 to 8 percent EBIT, below upstream OEMs (10 to 15 percent EBIT) and wholesalers (5 to 8 percent on larger scale), level with facilities management (5 to 7 percent); the pools sit at the ends of the chain. Instalco sits in the middle by choice and has no plan to leave it.
  • Fragility test: three of the five supports (volume, pricing relief, distressed sellers) are functions of the same cycle; two (labour barrier, regulation) are structural.
C-84

Historical precedents, rules of thumb and evidence on how sector companies out- and under-perform, and the fit.

C56

Question asked What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

Judgement: the sector's record says installers outperform in share price and earnings through four mechanisms, and Instalco currently fits the first two and is attempting the third; it underperforms through four failure modes, and Instalco carries the ingredients of two of them. The sector digest's rule of thumb for this exact cohort and moment is the one to build the model around: "in a building turn, volume returning to a pruned cost base drives earnings, not price; revenue lags construction starts by several quarters; the leaders have largely re-rated and now need earnings to arrive."

How they outperform:

  1. Margin-over-volume into the downturn, then volume returning onto a pruned cost base. Precedents: Bravida Denmark from 0.1 percent (Q2 2024) to 5.1 percent (H1 2026) under a CEO who took the divisional job himself; Bravida's own cost base about 90 percent variable and headcount cut 4 percent, Q2 2026 incremental adjusted EBITA of SEK 74m on SEK 655m of revenue (about 11 percent); Instalco's own Q2 2026, adjusted EBITA +SEK 38m against prior-year adjusted on +SEK 362m of revenue (about 14 percent incremental). Fit: Instalco fits this leg and is in it.
  2. Serial acquisition at 3 to 8x EV/EBITA rolled into a listed multiple of 10 to 14x, funded from cash. Precedents: SPIE (about 90 deals since 2013, 17 percent ROCE on 2019 to 2022 deals, over 25 percent post-tax RONIC, margin 6.7 to 7.6 percent in two years); Instalco itself (31 percent sales CAGR 2016 to 2020 at a 7.8 percent average margin; 150-plus deals at 3 to 6x; ROCE 18.2 percent in 2021); Green Landscaping under the same founder (26 percent CAGR, 4 to 6x). Rule of thumb: Nordic serial acquirers re-rate to 13 to 14.5x when the engine runs and sit at 10.2 to 12.2x when it is frozen (DNB Carnegie). Fit: Instalco's engine is frozen; the stock has nonetheless re-rated toward the running multiple (12.9x 24-month-forward P/E, 10.9x 2027e EV/EBITA on the broker's figure) on the first mechanism.
  3. Mix shift toward service, maintenance and consulting. Precedents: SPIE (70 to 75 percent of revenue from servicing existing assets, 85 percent start-of-year visibility, 108 percent cash conversion on negative working capital); Bravida (47 to 48 percent service, service growing 8 percent in the 2024 downturn while installation fell 3 percent); Fasadgruppen's Clear Line (30 to 40 percent margins on regulated remediation). Fit: Instalco's service share rose from 30 percent to 35 to 37 percent; Intec and Inmatiq are the consulting leg; it remains an installation-heavy book (new production 30 percent, renovation 33 percent).
  4. Capturing a structural end-market with a specialised capability (data centres, grids, nuclear). Precedents: Bravida's SEK 7bn-plus of data-centre backlog shifting it "from a cyclical recovery to a structural growth story"; SPIE's German grid work at 15 to 16 percent margins; Comfort Systems USA in the United States. Fit: none; Instalco is a generalist.

How they underperform:

  1. Project write-downs from mispriced fixed-price work, usually discovered late under percentage-of-completion accounting. Precedents: Caverion EUR 59m, Eltel EUR 55m, Sweco SEK 120m, Bravida Denmark 2023 to 2024 and Finland Q3 2025 (margin 0.6 percent), Instalco Norway 2021 (two largest subsidiaries cut 30 percent). Fit: Instalco carries 43 percent fixed-price work, SEK 25m to 75m projects, a decentralised structure that "makes internal control difficult", and a record of exactly this failure.
  2. Buying growth with debt and equity while organic growth stalls, the "false friend" roll-up. Precedent: Fasadgruppen (32 percent five-year revenue CAGR, 1 percent organic, margins 11.9 to 5.5 percent, leverage to 5.3x, a 38 percent-dilutive rights issue, CEO out). Fit: Instalco's acquired growth fell from 14.3 percent to 0.7 percent while organic turned negative, leverage reached 3.3x, ROCE fell to 9.6 percent and goodwill is 145 percent of equity; the research's own Analysis of Competing Hypotheses names "false friend" the winning profile. The difference from Fasadgruppen is that Instalco stopped buying, cut the dividend, and its organic growth has turned; the ingredients are present, the outcome is not.
  3. Governance failure in a decentralised model. Precedent: Bravida's Region Skåne over-invoicing (convictions, cancelled frameworks, a 10 percent one-day fall). Fit: shared model, no incident.
  4. Chasing volume into a price war or a mega-project beyond capability. Precedents: Bilfinger's September 2026 warning; Bravida's atNorth principal-contractor risk. Fit: low; Instalco's SEK 75m ceiling is its discipline.

Share-price rules of thumb from the files: the market pays for the recovery before the earnings (Instalco +63 percent and Bravida +61 percent in twelve months, target prices up 34 percent in six months while FY2026 EPS was cut 5.7 percent); a private bid or a scandal moves the group; "a sell-side target raised on multiple alone, with estimates flat or cut, is the signature of a priced recovery."

C-32

Customer demand: how far does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C58

Question asked How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

Judgement: supportive, with the fragility concentrated in the 48 percent of sales that go to construction companies, which are price-led and carry counterparty risk, and in the private and residential segments (Swedish residential new-build 7 percent of sales, Finnish residential frozen). The public and industrial half of the book is firm and growing.

  • Supportive: backlog +17 percent with Sweden +13.1 percent and Norway +40.9 percent; metropolitan projects moving into execution; customer enquiries rising; consulting demand strengthening (a leading indicator); 80 percent repeat customers; about 2,000 customers with the top five at 14 percent and none above 5 percent; service 35 percent and renovation 33 percent of sales, both low-volatility segments; demand "underpinned by resilient public sector investments" in Norway; Finnish industrial clients sustaining demand almost alone.
  • Fragile: "customer decision-making processes remain slow" (management, Q1 2026); "signs of market stabilisation remain highly uneven"; traditional process industries cautious on capital spending; Norwegian commercial real estate under deteriorating credit conditions (Norges Bank); construction companies delay payment in high-rate environments (Caverion); the Northvolt write-down shows what one customer's failure costs (SEK 64m, about 7 percent of FY2025 adjusted EBITA).
C-33

Route to market and intermediaries: helping or hindering, and how fragile?

❌ D47

Question asked To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

Judgement: the route is direct business-to-business, so the "intermediaries" are the general contractors who hold 48 percent of Instalco's sales and the wholesalers who supply the materials. The contractors hinder (price-led tenders, bargaining power in downturns, slow payment); the wholesalers currently help (absorbing inflation) but hold structural power; partnering contracts and multi-year frameworks are the company's way of converting a hindering route into a helping one, and they cover a growing but still minority share.

  • Contractors: NCC, Skanska, Peab, JM and the public sector are both the largest customers and the price-setters; Skanska "prioritises installation company size and experience for projects exceeding $10m", which favours scale; "large developers increasingly mandate unified single-brand contracts", which disfavours a federation. The Skanska 2026 to 2032 framework and the NCC renovation subcontract show the relationship route working.
  • Partnering: 57 percent of sales now on cost-plus or collaborative forms with transparent budgets and mark-ups, up from 54 percent in 2021; the SEK 1bn-plus NCC hospital partnering contract (2018) is the template. This is the single most important structural de-risking of the route and the model should hold it at 55 to 60 percent.
  • Wholesalers: see C-39.
  • Fragility: when private volumes fall, contractors "flood back into the stable renovation and infrastructure segments" and the route reverts to fifteen-bidder tenders.
C-34

Existing competitors' behaviour: helping or hurting, and how fragile is Instalco's position against them?

C50

Question asked How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

Judgement: helping in the near term, hurting over the medium term. Bravida is absorbing scarce labour into SEK 7bn-plus of data-centre contracts and pivoting to growth, which lifts pricing for everyone in the mid-sized market; Assemblin Caverion is integrating rather than attacking; Fasadgruppen is in repair. Over the medium term the same behaviours hurt: Bravida and Assemblin Caverion are building data-centre and digital capabilities Instalco lacks, private-equity roll-ups are bidding up Nordic and German target multiples, and a volume-led Nimlas with a SEK 40bn ambition is a price risk from the top.

  • Helping now: Bravida's order intake +44 percent on data centres; its 20/80 own-to-subcontractor model on those projects absorbs subcontractor capacity; its Swedish restructuring and branch closures in the south reduced competing capacity; Fasadgruppen cut business units by ten and is rejecting low-margin work.
  • Hurting later: Assemblin Caverion's dedicated data-centre business area (August 2026); Bravida's SEK 1bn automation business and 5D building information modelling; InstallatørGruppen's 48 acquisitions and Nimlas's expansion into Germany and Poland; experts observe centralised brands are "often preferred over federations" by large developers.
  • Fragility of Instalco's position: medium. Its local relationships and higher margin (7.1 percent against Bravida's 5.9 percent and Assemblin Caverion's 7.9 percent) are durable; its ability to respond to a volume-led attack is limited by leverage.
C-35

New entrants (adjacent, standard, orthogonal): extent and threat of each type.

C50

Question asked To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

Judgement: adjacent entrants are the live threat (newbuild contractors moving into renovation and public work during the downturn; facilities-management groups CBRE, JLL, Coor and ISS moving from soft to hard facilities management and explicitly intending to buy local installers; equipment makers expanding into services); standard entrants (private-equity roll-ups) threaten the acquisition arbitrage rather than the tender; orthogonal entrants (software, artificial intelligence, prefabrication) are tools, not competitors. Overall threat: moderate, cyclical, and most acute at the price of targets.

  • Adjacent: the renovation crowding is documented at Balco, Fasadgruppen and Instalco; it unwinds as newbuild recovers. The facilities-management push is a medium-term threat to the service share (35 percent) and is the one the Bravida research calls "the credible integration threat".
  • Standard: Nimlas (over 140 companies), InstallatørGruppen (48 deals), Brookfield in Germany (15 since 2023), Francks Kylindustri; purchase multiples "inflated from 4 to 6x EBITDA to over 8 to 10x in crowded markets" (former Lagercrantz executive).
  • Orthogonal: entry into physical installation by software or foreign contractors is blocked by licensing, local references and language (Ramboll, Sweco, Indra expert calls); foreign entrants must buy a niche platform, which makes them standard entrants.
C-36

Substitutes or alternatives: threat and direction.

C66

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement: low and not rising. The customer's alternatives to a technical installer are to self-perform (general contractors lack the licensed capability and local labour law makes it impractical), to buy equipment direct and hire labour (happening only in hyperscale data centres, where Instalco does not operate), or to use prefabricated modules (which installers themselves supply). Digital building automation adds installer content rather than replacing it. The one substitution that has happened is of the installer's brand by the contractor's framework, which is a route-to-market point, not a substitute.

C-37

Regulators and political actors: helping or hurting, and how fragile?

C57

Question asked What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

Judgement: helping on demand (the renovation directive, subsidies, defence and public budgets, municipal decarbonisation mandates, green lending rules), hurting mildly on cost (CSRD reporting, labour-crime compliance) and materially on the discount rate (the Riksbank). Fragile on timing: every member state missed the directive's deadline, Norway's government fell over EU energy rules, Sweden's ROT deduction was cut back in 2026, and "political opposition and severe budget constraints may cause regional authorities to dilute the enforcement of renovation standards" (Morgan Stanley). The direction is set; the dates are not.

C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting, how, and how fragile do they leave the company?

C50

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement: the forces act on demand (data centres and electrification pulling capacity; the directive making renovation non-discretionary), on competitors (digital platforms and prefabrication widening the gap between scaled and tail; private-equity capital widening the gap between funded and unfunded consolidators), on suppliers (wholesaler consolidation; transformer and switchgear lead times of 128 to 160 weeks) and on labour (scarcity lifting wages and apprentice economics). They act least on regulators and customers directly. Instalco's fragility is on two of these: labour (a 2027 wage round on un-indexed Swedish contracts; lock-up expiries) and digital (behind Bravida and SPIE). On demand and suppliers the forces are neutral to favourable.

  • The reinforcing loop the sector digest names, that scarce crews are "both the pricing power and the volume cap", applies: Instalco's organic growth is capped by hiring, and its pricing is floored by the same scarcity. The Consumer should model organic growth above about 8 percent as needing acquired headcount.
C-39

Suppliers: support or source of risk?

❌ D46

Question asked Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

Judgement: a structural source of risk that is currently quiet. The Nordic wholesaler layer is concentrated (Ahlsell 30 percent of Sweden, Dahl 41 percent of Norwegian HVAC, Sonepar, Rexel and Onninen 82 percent of Swedish electrical distribution; manufacturers sell about 80 percent of output through distributors) and passes commodity inflation downstream with a lag against which 43 percent of Instalco's book is fixed-price. In 2026 the layer is absorbing cost (Solar's margin squeeze), so the net is a slight tailwind; in a metals spike it becomes a headwind inside one or two quarters.

  • Instalco's mitigants: central agreements cover about 30 percent of purchases (Burkhalter's cover about 80 percent, for contrast); subsidiaries buy locally for responsiveness; direct-to-site delivery with no inventory (SEK 190m); index clauses in cost-plus work; a supplier code of conduct signed by all prioritised central suppliers. Disintermediation is low because small orders cannot be shipped economically from manufacturers.
  • Subcontractors (16 percent of costs) are a capacity valve, "not inherently margin-dilutive" (DNB Carnegie on Bravida), but a source of execution and compliance risk (SPIE's Cologne precedent; labour-crime enforcement).
  • Data gap: no figure for Instalco's own exposure to any single wholesaler; the research treats the group as "structurally insulated" by subsidiary-level purchasing, which is true of concentration and untrue of pricing.

Company - Competitive advantage

❌ D457 questions
C-4147C-4242C-4370C-4455C-4540C-4635C-4740

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The advantage is real, local and not widening: Instalco's retained-brand, founder-run subsidiaries earn repeat work and a margin above the tail and above Bravida, but the moat is relational rather than structural, it carries no pricing power at the tender, it is behind the leaders on digital and specialised capability, and it depends each period on management's intervention rather than on any mechanical drift. The market is huge relative to the company and the runway is capital-limited. For the model this means a margin ceiling at the company's own history (8 percent), reached by utilisation and bad-debt normalisation and not by price, and an organic growth rate bounded by hiring.

C-41

Sustainable relative advantages or moats, their strength, and whether widening or narrowing.

❌ D47

Question asked Which sustainable relative competitive advantages or moats does the company hold (customer-related; production-related; scale-based; regulatory or political; culture or knowledge), how strong is each, and are they widening or narrowing?

Judgement: Instalco holds one real moat (local relational lock-in through retained brands and founder-managers), two partial ones (cluster bidding across adjacent subsidiaries; a culture that retains people better than the centralised peer) and two that the research itself discounts (procurement scale; entrepreneur incentive alignment, which erodes at lock-up expiry). None is widening; the digital and data-centre capability gap to Bravida is narrowing Instalco's relative position; the moat is adequate to hold a margin above the tail's and below the best European operators'.

  • Customer-related (strong, stable): acquired firms keep their names and their founder-CEO's direct relationships with regional developers; 80 percent of revenue is repeat and about 80 percent of contracts come through local relationships; customers choose "proven competence and clear local counterparts over pure bidding price" (former Skanska developer). Switching costs are low between projects and high within one (restarted approvals, retraining on provider procedures). Strength: this is why the group earned 8 percent when peers earned 5.5 percent. Trend: flat; the countervailing expert view is that large developers "increasingly mandate unified single-brand contracts".
  • Production-related (partial): clusters of subsidiaries submit combined bids (the SEK 100m Gothenburg Central Station tender won with eight subsidiaries; LKAB sites; NCC hospital contracts to three Instalco companies); adjacent units share electricians and pipefitters. Strength: moderate, local. Trend: flat.
  • Scale-based (weak): central agreements cover about 30 percent of purchases; a Caverion strategy expert says subcontractor and procurement advantages are "often exaggerated" and decentralised consolidators rarely run a unified procurement platform. Strength: weak. Trend: flat; Bravida's purchasing platform widens its lead.
  • Regulatory (shared): licensing of high-voltage electricians and HVAC technicians bars entry for everyone equally; not a relative advantage.
  • Culture and knowledge (partial, at risk): eNPS 31 against Bravida's 14; turnover 15.8 percent against 20.4 percent; the Instalco Academy trains project managers; experts say Instalco's acquired firms "require significantly less central support". Trend: narrowing, because turnover rose from 13.1 to 15.8 percent over two years and the baseline tightens autonomy.
  • The research's own summary is the right one: the decentralised structure is "simultaneously the foundational moat and the single largest execution risk".
C-42

Scale relative to competitors, and what it confers.

❌ D42

Question asked How large is the company's scale relative to competitors and other actors (revenue, headcount and so on), and what does that scale confer?

Judgement: mid-sized. SEK 13.6bn of revenue against Bravida SEK 28.2bn, Assemblin Caverion about SEK 41bn, SPIE EUR 10.4bn; about 150 subsidiaries of around 30 to 50 employees each; roughly 7,000 employees on the backlog-per-employee figure (SEK 10,937m at SEK 1.57m per FTE). Scale confers less in this industry than density does: the gains are in recruiting (technicians prefer large employers and large projects), in cluster bids, in a listed currency for acquisitions and in spreading a very small central cost. It does not confer purchasing leverage (see C-41) or pricing power. Instalco's scale is sufficient for the mid-sized project band it chooses and insufficient for the data-centre and hospital turnkey band Bravida and Assemblin Caverion are moving into.

  • What the scale buys that is measurable: capex 0.69 percent of sales; a SEK 3.4bn syndicated facility; investment-grade-style bank support (Danske, SEB, Svensk Exportkredit); a USD 2.9m-a-day listed share that is itself the constraint on institutional ownership (crowded after +63 percent).
  • What it does not buy: revenue per employee that matches Bravida's (the research says Instalco "lagged Bravida on revenue per full-time employee" and managed utilisation swings worse).
C-43

Realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and the mid-term growth it allows.

C70

Question asked How large is the realistic total addressable market relative to the company, and what rate of growth does it allow for over the mid term?

Judgement: very large relative to the company and growing at about 3 percent real; the constraint on Instalco's growth is capital and labour, not market. Nordic technical installation is about SEK 209bn (Sweden SEK 141bn), so Instalco's SEK 13.6bn is about 6.5 percent; Germany adds EUR 100bn to 120bn against Fabri's EUR 117m. Organic growth of 3 to 5 percent through the cycle plus acquired growth of 5 to 10 percent a year is what the market allows; the company's own 10 percent target is a statement about its funding, not its runway.

  • Market growth: Nordic light construction grew at an inflation-fuelled 8 percent a year 2019 to 2024, with renovation +8 percent a year and newbuild -5 percent a year over 2021 to 2024; long-term real growth about 3 percent to 2029 and renovation 4 percent (DNB Carnegie); Nordic technical consulting 7.4 percent a year 2018 to 2022; the German installation market 3 percent nominal to 2030 at Burkhalter's Swiss analogue; the directive could lift renovation rates toward 3 percent of stock after 2027.
  • The runway in targets: about 25,000 Nordic firms and 91,300 German ones; 1,000 to 1,250 identified targets in Switzerland alone for comparison; purchase multiples 3 to 6x at home, 7 to 9x in Germany.
  • The research's one saturation caveat (RedEye): Nordic installation acquisitions "reach density limits" in core verticals, which is why Instalco has moved into consulting, automation, scaffolding and Germany. That is a point about the home market's remaining attractive targets, not about demand.
C-44

Surplus customer utility against alternatives: leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior?

C55

Question asked How high is the company's surplus customer utility against competing or alternative offerings, and is it leading, or at least demonstrably not inferior? (Hard to prove, easier to disprove; the aim is to disprove inferiority at Early-Stage and Deep Dive, for example Sophos, Bytes.)

Judgement: demonstrably not inferior; not demonstrably leading. The evidence that disproves inferiority is repeat business from the largest Nordic buyers (NCC, Skanska, Peab, JM since the 1980s, LKAB, Boliden, public regions), an 80 percent repeat rate, expert testimony that Instalco "secures more high-performing subsidiaries from the outset" than centralised peers, and a margin history above peers that could only come from customers paying for something. The evidence against leadership is that Bravida's digital tools and brand are preferred by some large developers, that the group's brands are invisible outside their regions, and that public buyers choose on price.

  • The "Sophos, Bytes" test the question cites is whether an incumbent can be shown inferior; here no customer-side evidence shows it, and the Skanska 2026 to 2032 framework and NCC's Stockholm award in 2026 are current repeat awards from the most demanding buyers.
C-45

Demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of surplus utility, by application not conjecture.

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent does the company have, and use, demonstrable pricing power with minimal erosion of its relative surplus customer utility, shown by evidence of application rather than conjecture?

Judgement: weak at the tender stage, moderate in service and partnering, and not demonstrated by application in the last cycle. The company's "pricing power" is exercised as the power to decline work (selective bidding) and to pass materials through index clauses, not as the power to raise like-for-like prices; the margin fell from 8.2 percent to 5.9 percent through the 2022 to 2025 inflation, which is the application record. The Consumer should model pricing as a floor-holding mechanism (no margin loss to materials in 57 percent of the book) and not as a margin driver.

  • Applied evidence for: index-linked contracts passing material price rises; partnering with transparent mark-ups; the shift from 46 to 43 percent fixed-price; "very limited" inflation impact in Q1 2026; service work not contractually bound and repriceable; Finnish industrial contracts earning 12 percent at high utilisation.
  • Applied evidence against: 15 bidders; public buyers "heavily prioritise the lowest price"; Swedish contracts lack automatic indexation (Danish AB18 contracts have it); Swedish legacy contracts signed in 2024 to 2025 "still working their way through the backlog" at low margins; "40 percent of contractors are pushing back on price hikes" sector-wide.
  • Peer contrast: SPIE "priced ahead of labour inflation" with daily pricing tools and indexation, growing revenue per employee faster than staff costs; Burkhalter passes metal cost "via dynamic contract pricing" in a labour-scarce market. Instalco has neither the tooling nor the market position for that.
C-46

Sharing scale economies with customers, stated or de facto.

❌ D35

Question asked To what extent does the company share its scale economies with customers, stated or de facto, in its messaging, strategy, culture or values?

Judgement: not a feature of the model. Instalco keeps what little central purchasing saving it has; its de facto sharing happens inside partnering contracts, where transparent cost-plus economics pass procurement savings to the customer by construction, and in service relationships where continuity is the value. There is no messaging, strategy or cultural statement about sharing scale with customers; the strategy is explicitly to capture margin. This is neither unusual nor wrong in a 5 to 8 percent margin trade, but it means no scale-economies-shared flywheel exists to model.

C-47

Gravitational or mechanical forces improving advantage and outputs each period, versus heroic dynamism.

❌ D40

Question asked To what extent do gravitational or mechanical forces, rather than heroic company dynamism, improve the company's competitive advantage and its financial outputs against peers with each period that passes?

Judgement: the improvement is heroic, not gravitational. The research says it in terms: near-term margin expansion "relies on heroic operational interventions by the central executive team". Three mechanical forces do run in Instalco's favour with time (regulation making renovation non-discretionary; labour scarcity flooring prices; the acquisition arbitrage when the engine is on), but none of them improves Instalco's position against peers each period, because every scaled peer enjoys them and the better-capitalised ones enjoy them more. The one mechanical lever that is Instalco's own is the Fabri option ladder, which raises its share of a 12.7 percent-margin business on dates already set.

  • Against gravitational: the margin recovery requires the founder's presence (he has tied his tenure to it), the baseline's enforcement, lock-up retention and selective bidding every quarter; the moment attention lapses the tail's pricing re-enters. Turnover rising to 15.8 percent is the sign that the system does not hold itself.
  • Toward gravitational: once leverage is below 2.5x and bolt-ons resume at 3 to 6x into a 12 to 14x listed multiple, each deal adds about 10 basis points of margin and compounds without heroics (SPIE's "accretive bolt-ons typically add around 10 basis points of annual consolidated margin expansion"); that is the state the company is trying to return to, not the state it is in.

Company - Economic strength

❌ D4911 questions
C-4956C-5054C-5144C-5254C-5360C-5436C-7545C-7640C-7747C-7860C-7944

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Economically Instalco is a capital-light, cash-generative operating business attached to a leaky and levered holding structure. The operating economics are good (capex 0.7 percent of sales, free cash flow above net income, an incremental margin on returning volume of 10 to 14 percent, a margin ceiling of 8 percent demonstrated for five years) and the recovery arithmetic is strong (SEK 220m to 300m of EBITA from utilisation, bad debt and mix; consensus EPS +29, +32, +14 percent). Below EBITA the base is not clean: recurring "non-recurring" items, SEK 132m of amortisation, a 28 percent tax rate, net financial items at 14 to 20 percent of EBIT and rising minorities take 37 percent off adjusted EPS to reach reported; leverage at 2.6 to 2.8x amplifies both the recovery and the risk and sits in a regime that refuses it; returns on capital employed are 9.6 percent and recover only with margin. Cash conversion, the output that gates everything else, has gone the wrong way for one quarter and will be judged on the next two prints. For the model: build from adjusted EBITA with explicit amortisation, tax at 26 to 28 percent, minorities rising, net financial items flat rather than falling, and a cash-conversion assumption that is 100 percent in the base case only if Q4 2026 prints it.

C-49

Earnings-per-share growth, with what revenue growth, and how durable?

C56

Question asked What earnings-per-share growth is the company generating, with what revenue growth, and how durable is it?

Judgement: the three brokers expect adjusted EPS of SEK 2.28, 3.01 and 3.42 for FY2026 to FY2028 from SEK 1.76 in FY2025 (+29, +32, +14 percent) on revenue of SEK 15,193m, 17,171m and 17,863m (+11.7, +13.0, +4.0 percent). About half of the three-year EPS growth is margin recovery, a quarter is Fabri's consolidation, and the rest is falling net financial items; durability is high for the first leg (restoring the company's own history), medium for the second (a 51 percent-owned German business at 12.7 percent with 49 percent leaking to minorities) and low for the third (it assumes deleveraging into falling rates while the Riksbank is hiking). After FY2028, growth reverts to 7 to 8 percent unless the acquisition engine restarts, and the latest memo's own path sits 3, 7 and 8 percent below consensus for the three years, almost entirely on margin.

  • The revenue bridge: FY2026 +11.7 percent is about 8 percent organic (H1 2026 organic averaged 6.7 percent, H2 faces trough comparatives) plus 3 to 4 percent from Fabri's four to five consolidated months (about SEK 1.3bn annualised; a one-month reporting lag); FY2027 +13.0 percent is Fabri's incremental seven months (about 5 points) plus 6 to 8 percent organic, which the segment consensus confirms (Sweden SEK 10,416m to 11,089m, +6.5 percent; Norway +8.5 percent; Finland and Germany +42 percent on Fabri) and which is a demanding organic assumption against comparatives that harden from Q1 2027; FY2028 +4.0 percent is organic only, which tells the Consumer the brokers have not modelled a bolt-on restart in their revenue lines. Pareto's FY2028 revenue (SEK 18,129m) is the only one above the others.
  • The margin bridge (brokers' basis): EBITDA margin 9.0 percent (FY2025) to 9.8, 10.3, 10.6 percent; EBIT SEK 936m, 1,198m, 1,303m (6.2, 7.0, 7.3 percent of revenue), which with about SEK 135m of amortisation of acquired intangibles is EBITA of roughly 7.0, 7.8 and 8.0 percent: the company's target is in the FY2028 line.
  • Below EBIT: pre-tax profit SEK 702m, 1,027m, 1,170m implies net financial items of about SEK 234m, 171m and 133m; net income SEK 527m, 772m, 865m implies tax plus minorities of about 25 percent; shares flat at about 268 to 270m. So EPS growth in FY2027 (+32 percent) is EBIT +28 percent amplified by financial items falling SEK 63m; in FY2028 (+14 percent) it is EBIT +9 percent amplified by financial items falling SEK 38m. The gearing of EPS to EBIT is about 1.2 to 1.5x in these years; it works in reverse if rates rise.
  • History for scale: reported EPS SEK 1.51 (FY2019), 2.10 (FY2021), 2.29 (FY2023), 1.28 (FY2025); brokers'-basis EPS SEK 2.67 (FY2023), 1.81, 1.76. Consensus FY2027 (SEK 3.01) is 13 percent above the FY2023 peak on 20 percent more revenue, which is a margin-and-leverage recovery, not a new level.
  • Durability test: the first leg has the company's own precedent (above 8 percent for five years under the same CEO); the Fabri leg is contractual but 49 percent-shared; the financial-items leg is contestable (C-07); the post-2028 leg is unmodelled by consensus and depends on leverage below 2.5x.
C-50

Immediate scope to deploy capital at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years; implications for potential and fair multiple.

C54

Question asked How much immediate scope is there to deploy capital or operating spend at a high return on new invested capital for three to five years, and what does that mean for mid- and long-term potential and the fair multiple?

Judgement: large in principle, gated in practice. The deployment opportunity is bolt-on acquisitions at 3 to 6x EV/EBITA in the Nordics (a 17 to 33 percent pre-tax yield on the purchase price, before any multiple arbitrage) and 7 to 9x in Germany (11 to 14 percent), from a pipeline of tens of thousands of firms; the gate is leverage, which management has set at 2.5x and which stood at 2.6x at Q2 2026. The historical run-rate was about SEK 800m a year of consideration adding SEK 1.1bn to 1.7bn of sales; the sell-side's base case restarts that in 2027 for about SEK 56m of EBITA a year (0.3 to 0.4 points of margin). The fair multiple follows the gate: 13 to 14.5x EV/EBITA with the engine running, 10 to 12x with it off (the Nordic serial-acquirer precedent).

  • Operating spend with high returns: minimal; capex is 0.7 percent of sales; the organic start-ups (Intec, Inmatiq) are the only operating deployments and they are small.
  • The record on returns: ROCE 18.2 percent in FY2021 at the peak of the engine, 13.8 percent in FY2023, 9.6 percent in FY2025 with the engine off and margins down; the fall is margin, not purchase price. Fabri's step 2 at EUR 35.7m for 27 percent implies about EUR 132m of equity value for 100 percent, roughly 9x to 12x Fabri's 2025 EBITA (EUR 14.9m pro forma on EUR 117m of sales, or EUR 10.9m on the reported EUR 86m, both at 12.7 percent) before any debt inside Fabri; DNB Carnegie put the initial stake at under 10x. Either way the German entry was bought at a fuller price than the Nordic bolt-ons at 3 to 6x and the arbitrage there is thinner.
  • Mid- and long-term potential: if leverage reaches the broker's 2.3x by end-2026 and 1.6x by 2027, about SEK 800m a year of deployment is self-funded from FY2027 free cash flow (consensus SEK 1,101m) after dividends (SEK 240m), which is the compounding case; if cash conversion stays at 90 percent, the engine stays off through 2027 and the multiple's engine-on branch closes.
C-51

Returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital.

❌ D44

Question asked How high are the returns on capital: post-tax return on new invested capital, on capital employed and on tangible capital?

Judgement: low on capital employed (ROCE 9.6 percent in FY2025 against 18.2 percent in FY2021 and above 20 percent at Addtech and Lagercrantz), low on equity (ROE 11.7 percent from 25.1 percent), very high on tangible capital (capex 0.7 percent of sales, tangible assets about SEK 890m against SEK 13.6bn of sales) and high on new invested capital when the engine runs (bolt-ons at 3 to 6x EV/EBITA give a post-tax RONIC in the mid-teens to mid-twenties before arbitrage; SPIE's comparable programme earned 17 percent ROCE and over 25 percent post-tax RONIC). The gap between tangible and total returns is goodwill (SEK 5,210m, 145 percent of equity), which is the price of the roll-up.

  • Implication for the model: returns on capital employed recover with margin almost one for one (the capital base is static); each point of EBITA margin on SEK 15bn of sales is about SEK 150m of EBIT, about 2 points of ROCE on roughly SEK 7bn of capital employed. At the 8 percent target ROCE returns toward 14 to 15 percent, not to the 18 percent of 2021 unless acquisitions resume at the old multiples.
C-52

Conversion of net income into free cash flow; behaviour of net working capital and similar items.

C54

Question asked How high is the conversion of net income into free cash flow, and how do net working capital and similar items behave?

Judgement: structurally high, seasonally violent, and currently in question. On FactSet's definition free cash flow was 217 percent of reported net income in FY2025 (SEK 744m against SEK 343m) because net income is depressed by amortisation, interest and tax while capex is tiny; on the company's definition cash conversion was 108 percent in FY2025 and 90 percent rolling at Q2 2026. Working capital builds in Q2 and Q3 and releases in Q4 (SEK 451m in Q4 2025), so quarterly readings mislead; the question the Q3 and Q4 2026 prints must answer is whether the Q2 build (receivables SEK 2,009m, contract assets SEK 808m from SEK 551m) is the usual seasonal pattern on a growing book or the structural consumption that growth plus 43 percent fixed-price work implies. The key-question analysis's verdict, which I adopt, is a composite: Swedish late-quarter invoicing that reverses, and Norwegian long-duration public projects that bind capital for years.

  • The numbers: operating cash flow SEK 495m (FY2019) to SEK 1,010m (FY2025); FactSet free cash flow SEK 412m to SEK 744m; net working capital on the company's definition 2.8 percent of sales (2022 peak), 2.3 percent (2024), 1.6 percent (end-2025), 1.1 percent (Q1 2026), 2.3 percent (Q2 2026); on FactSet's gross definition (receivables plus inventories less payables) SEK 1,652m or 11.7 percent of sales at Q2 2026, within a 10.1 to 13.8 percent range over eleven quarters, so the gross measure shows no structural drift. Consensus operating cash flow SEK 1,048m, 1,370m, 1,477m and free cash flow SEK 672m, 1,101m, 1,210m for FY2026 to FY2028, which assume conversion returns to about 100 percent from FY2027.
  • Peers for the base rate: Bravida 77 to 79 percent on large-project milestones, SPIE 108 percent on negative working capital of 34 days, Fasadgruppen 26 percent in H1 2026. Instalco's receivables days (90 in 2024) were 27 percent better than Bravida's (124).
  • "Similar items" the Consumer must carry: acquisitions (SEK 316m in FY2025, SEK 56m in H1 2026, the Fabri step in Q3 2026), lease payments (about SEK 505m of lease liabilities), dividends (SEK 183m in FY2025; SEK 134m paid in Q2 2026), contingent consideration and the Fabri option (undisclosed by segment), interest (SEK 167m). FactSet's free cash flow deducts none of acquisitions, leases or earn-outs.
  • Thresholds from the memo and the research: Q3 operating cash flow recovering toward SEK 200m; receivables below SEK 1,800m; contract assets below SEK 800m; rolling conversion at or above 100 percent at Q4 2026 and leverage below 2.5x. Below 90 percent at Q4 2026 invalidates the cash leg.
C-53

Gross and operating margins, level and trend.

C60

Question asked How high are gross and operating profit margins, and how are they trending?

Judgement: operating margins are mid-cycle for the company and above most Nordic peers, trending up from a 2025 trough; gross margin is not a meaningful line for a contractor and FactSet's "gross profit" is in fact its adjusted EBIT (both SEK 558m, 4.1 percent, in FY2025). The lines to model are adjusted EBITA (6.4 percent FY2025, 7.1 percent Q2 2026, 8 percent target), reported EBITA (5.9 percent), and the brokers' EBITDA (9.0 percent FY2025, consensus 9.8, 10.3, 10.6 percent).

  • History: adjusted EBITA 8.8 percent (2020), 8.2 percent (2021), 7.6 percent (2023), 6.4 percent (2025); reported EBITA 5.9 percent (2025); FactSet operating EBITDA 10.2 percent (2019 to 2020) falling to 8.0 percent (2025); FactSet adjusted EBIT 8.5 percent (2019) to 4.1 percent (2025), the steepest line because amortisation of acquired intangibles (SEK 132m in FY2025, SEK 189m in FY2024) and depreciation grew with the roll-up.
  • Segments at Q2 2026: Sweden 7.0 percent (5.0 in Q1, 6.6 a year earlier, 9.5 percent in Q4 2021 at the peak), Norway 4.7 percent (Q1), Finland 8.1 percent (12.1 in Q1), Fabri 12.7 percent (2025). Sweden is 71 percent of sales and the whole story: DNB Carnegie's Swedish path is 7.2 percent in 2027 and 7.6 percent in 2028, below the group target, which the group reaches only with Finland, Norway and Germany above it.
  • Trend drivers in order: utilisation (revenue per employee +5 percent is one of the broker's two main levers), bad debt normalising (SEK 64m to 75m a year of items in 2024 to 2025), legacy low-price Swedish contracts rolling off (signed 2024 to 2025, "diminishing over the next several quarters"), mix (Fabri +0.4 points of group margin; service 35 percent), and against them the 2027 wage round and Finland normalising from 12 to 8 percent. The incremental adjusted EBITA margin in Q2 2026 was about 14 percent on the extra revenue; a 50 basis point margin move is about 8.9 percent of EPS (SEK 0.21).
  • Peer levels: Bravida 5.9 percent, Assemblin Caverion 7.9 percent, SPIE 7.6 percent (8.0 percent target 2028), Burkhalter 6.1 percent EBIT, Fasadgruppen 8.2 percent adjusted (2025) and 4.7 percent in H1 2026; small local Swedish firms 7 to 14 percent EBIT in good years.
C-54

Cleanliness of the earnings base: adjusted versus reported, one-offs, capitalised costs, tax and other leakage.

❌ D36

Question asked How clean is the earnings base: how large is the gap between adjusted and reported earnings; do one-offs, capitalised costs or other items flatter it; and does the tax rate or any other non-obvious leakage erode earnings per share?

Judgement: not clean, and the leakage is large and recurring. Between adjusted EBITA (SEK 870m) and reported basic EPS (SEK 1.28) in FY2025 sit SEK 75m of items affecting comparability, SEK 132m of amortisation of acquired intangibles, SEK 167m of net interest, a 28.1 percent tax rate and minorities; the brokers' adjusted EPS (SEK 1.76) is 37 percent above reported. The "non-recurring" items have recurred in every recent year (SEK 65m restructuring and SEK 29m goodwill in Q4 2024; SEK 64m Northvolt in Q1 2025; SEK 11m leadership in Q2 2025; SEK 75m for FY2025; the risks digest's unverified SEK 75m in Q1 2026). Capitalised costs are not an issue (capex 0.7 percent; no capitalised development). The tax rate is the under-examined leak: 20 to 22 percent in 2019 to 2023, 25.1 percent in 2024, 28.1 percent in 2025 against a 20.6 percent Swedish statutory rate, which points to non-deductible items and loss-making units; consensus assumes about 25 percent.

  • What the Consumer should adopt: model from adjusted EBITA, deduct amortisation of acquired intangibles explicitly (consensus D&A of SEK 402m, 358m, 348m for FY2026 to FY2028 shows it rolling off as old deals age, which is a genuine EPS tailwind on the reported line and nothing on the adjusted line), carry a recurring SEK 50m to 75m of "non-recurring" items in the reported case, carry minorities rising with Fabri (49 percent of roughly SEK 120m to 165m of annual EBITA depending on whether the reported or pro forma 2025 base is used, or about SEK 40m to 55m after Fabri's own interest and tax, growing as German bolt-ons land), and use a 26 to 28 percent tax rate rather than 25 percent until the company explains the 2025 rate.
  • Data gaps flagged by the memo: an unexplained SEK 105m between FactSet's adjusted EBIT and the company's adjusted EBITA for FY2025; a net income basis gap (SEK 343m FactSet against SEK 376m company); the FY2026 consensus range SEK 1.79 (Pareto) to SEK 2.30 (DNB Carnegie), which is partly a basis difference.
C-75

Fixed-cost operating leverage: how much a change in revenue moves operating profit, and the range it creates.

❌ D45

Question asked How material is the fixed-cost operating leverage: how much does a given change in revenue move operating profit, and how wide a range of outcomes does that create?

Judgement: low in the textbook sense and material in practice through utilisation. The cost base is about 92 percent variable (materials 40 percent, employees 36 percent, subcontractors 16 percent; central overhead a few tens of millions), so fixed-cost absorption is small; but installation labour is only variable with a lag (Swedish labour law is "highly protective of existing staff"), so in a volume fall margins drop through under-absorption of technicians and in a volume rise they recover through utilisation. The research's sensitivities put it at: 1 percent of organic revenue moves EPS by about 1.3 percent; a 2 percent volume decline cuts net earnings about 2.1 percent; 50 basis points of margin moves EPS 8.9 percent. The range this creates for 2027 EPS is SEK 1.80 (housing recovery delayed, margin stuck at 6.5 percent) to SEK 3.00-plus (8 percent organic growth, 8 percent margin) around a consensus SEK 3.01.

  • The incremental evidence: Q2 2026 adjusted EBITA rose SEK 38m on SEK 362m of extra revenue (about 10 to 14 percent incremental depending on basis), roughly twice the group margin; the Q4 2025 and Q2 2026 margins of 7.2 and 7.1 percent came on volumes still about 15 percent below normal. That is the operating leverage: utilisation of an installed labour base, with revenue per employee the metric. Finland showed the ceiling (12.1 percent at full utilisation) and the floor (2.0 percent a year earlier).
  • Peers: Bravida 90 percent variable, 42 percent "fully flexible" through subcontractors, Q2 2026 incremental margin about 11 percent; SPIE "the absence of operating leverage ... margins expand step-by-step through contract selectivity, mix and pricing rather than volume"; Fasadgruppen showed the reverse in Q1 2026 (0.5 percent margin on -12 percent organic).
  • For the model: treat incremental EBITA margin on organic revenue at 10 to 15 percent while volumes are below normal, falling to the group margin as utilisation normalises; treat a volume decline as hitting at the same ratio with a two-quarter lag; treat the 2027 wage round as a step in the variable cost that selectivity can only partly offset.
C-76

Financial leverage: how much it amplifies EPS and risk, and how comfortable it is.

❌ D40

Question asked How material is the financial leverage or gearing: how much does it amplify earnings per share and risk, and how comfortable is it?

Judgement: material and the second-largest swing in the EPS bridge after margin; comfortable against covenants, uncomfortable against the company's own target and against the regime. Net debt SEK 3,561m at Q2 2026 (FactSet, including leases), 2.6x on the company's definition and 2.82x on FactSet's; facilities SEK 3,850m with SEK 2,800m drawn; interest-bearing debt in local currencies at floating-style bank terms with a leverage-based margin step-up; net interest SEK 167m in FY2025 and SEK 63m in Q2 2026 (annualising above SEK 240m), 14 to 20 percent of EBIT. Each 100 basis points on about SEK 3.5bn is about SEK 35m before tax, about 4 percent of FY2026 consensus EPS; each 0.5x of leverage reduction at current rates is about SEK 25m to 30m of interest, about 3 percent of EPS. Consensus has net financial items falling from about SEK 234m (FY2026) to SEK 133m (FY2028); that is a 10-point contribution to the FY2027 EPS growth of 32 percent and depends on both deleveraging and no rate rise.

  • Comfort: covenants are met "with a wide margin" (the facility was extended in June 2026 to at least 2028); gearing 106.7 percent of equity; goodwill 145 percent of equity, so a write-down of a few percent of goodwill would take equity below net debt. DNB Carnegie's path is 2.3x by end-2026 and 1.6x by 2027; the Fabri step is "broadly leverage-neutral" on consolidation arithmetic; the pre-Q2 broker view was that leverage would rise to 2.7x in Q2 and it held at 2.6x.
  • Risk: the research's consistent downside is a dilutive equity raise if write-downs and weak cash coincide; the precedent is Fasadgruppen (rights issue at 38 percent dilution from 5.3x). Instalco's history includes issuing shares for acquisitions (SEK 138m targeted issue for Fabri step 1), so the market will not treat a raise as unthinkable. Peers: Bravida 1.3x, SPIE 2.1x after EUR 640m of acquisitions, Fasadgruppen 3.3x post-raise, Assemblin Caverion 3.3x.
  • Regime: the weekly digest's Fragility axis is Double Underweight and "the market refused leverage"; a levered small cap in a hiking cycle pays for leverage in its multiple as well as in interest.
C-77

How much of each major cost part is cuttable?

❌ D47

Question asked How much of each major part of the cost base is cuttable or reducible?

Judgement: little remains to cut, by design and because the 2024 cuts were made. Materials (about 40 percent of costs) are pass-through and not cuttable; subcontractors (about 16 percent) are the capacity valve and flex with volume automatically; own labour (about 36 percent) is cuttable only slowly under Swedish law and was cut by 110 employees and 100 subcontractors in late 2024; central overhead is already a few tens of millions on SEK 13.6bn of sales; eight loss-making subsidiaries were closed (SEK -30m of EBITA removed). The remaining "cut" is bad debt (SEK 64m to 75m a year of impairments in 2024 to 2025 against a normal level that DNB Carnegie expects to fall materially) and under-performing units that the baseline is meant to fix rather than close.

  • The one structural cost opportunity is back-office consolidation across 150 subsidiaries, which the broker names and which Burkhalter's Abacus migration and Bravida's three-into-one merger show is worth 0.3 to 0.5 points of margin at a centralised peer; Instalco's model limits it to shared services the subsidiaries choose to use.
C-78

Beyond cost: scope to improve financial outputs over the mid term; peer precedents; few big areas or many small; congruence with strategy.

C60

Question asked Beyond cutting costs, how much plausible scope is there to improve the financial outputs over the mid term; do peer precedents show it is achievable; would it come from a few big areas or many small ones; and is it congruent with the stated strategy?

Judgement: the scope is one big area (Sweden, 71 percent of sales, from 7.0 percent to the 8 percent-plus it earned in 2017 to 2021) and three small ones (Norway from under 5 percent, Finland holding 8 to 10 percent, Fabri at 12.7 percent lifting the mix), worth together about 1.5 to 2 points of group margin or SEK 220m to 300m of EBITA on FY2025 sales (a 25 to 35 percent increase in adjusted EBITA), to be reached by utilisation, bad-debt normalisation and legacy roll-off rather than by cost or price. Peer precedents say it is achievable and takes two to three years (Bravida Denmark two years; SPIE 90 basis points in two years; Instalco's own 2017 to 2021). It is entirely congruent with the stated strategy, which is nothing other than this.

  • The arithmetic the brokers use: restoring 8 percent on about SEK 15bn yields normalised EBITA of about SEK 1,200m against SEK 870m adjusted in FY2025 (DNB Carnegie's bridge: lower bad-debt provisions and revenue per employee +5 percent). Consensus FY2028 EBIT of SEK 1,303m plus amortisation is about SEK 1,440m of EBITA on SEK 17.9bn, about 8.0 percent, so consensus already carries the full scope by FY2028. The record's modal path reaches 8.0 percent only from FY2029 and carries 7.6 and 7.9 percent in FY2027 and FY2028.
  • What would add beyond the strategy: Inmatiq scaling (50 to 100 basis points by 2028, not in consensus); the bolt-on restart (0.3 to 0.4 points a year at SEK 800m of deployment, not in consensus revenue). What would subtract: the 2027 wage round, a Finland normalisation below 8 percent, a Norwegian write-down.
C-79

Narrowness of the business perimeter, its change, and what that means for predictability.

❌ D44

Question asked How narrow is the business perimeter (number of countries and business lines), how is it changing, and what does that mean for predictability?

Judgement: narrow by activity and wide by unit. Four countries (three until August 2026), five disciplines, one industry stage; but about 150 subsidiaries each with full profit-and-loss responsibility, about 6,000 active projects, 2,000 customers, and from Q3 2026 a merged "Finland and Germany" segment reported with a one-month lag. The perimeter is widening (Germany, scaffolding, automation). Predictability is medium at group level (service 35 percent, backlog 12 to 18 months, segment data restated to 2024) and low at the project level, where the research says a decentralised model is "inherently unanalysable with high precision" and where every one of 150 units is a place a fixed-price project can be mispriced. For the Consumer this means the group margin is forecastable within about a point and the quarterly reported line is not, because one SEK 50m write-down in one subsidiary moves a quarter's EBITA by 15 to 20 percent.

  • Disclosure gaps that limit predictability: backlog duration and its fixed-price share are not published; subsidiary-level write-downs are not disclosed; the ABC programme's uplift is not reported; contingent consideration by segment is not given; the Finland and Germany merger obscures both.
